####### DMA Entities Scoping

The Double Materiality Assessment (DMA) process incorporated a hybrid mode, combining a topdown approach with an assessment performed at the group level while engaging or consulting with subsidiaries in order, to receive the necessary information; and a bottom-up approach, with an assessment performed at the subsidiary level and consolidating the results at the group level. The companies' scope for consideration was parent company, subsidiaries, associates and joint operations. Investment entities were excluded from the process. Nevertheless, the investor companies to the relevant investees (i.e. IREON VENTURE, MOTOR OIL HELLAS SA, LPC, THALIS, VERD) have primarily participated in the DMA process.

####### Thresholding

MOTOR OIL Group employed a thresholding approach to prioritize sustainability impacts, risks and opportunities within its value chain. The methodology employed by MOTOR OIL Group is based on calculating an adjusted average to determine the threshold for top scores. This threshold is set by subtracting a fixed percentage from the overall average of all scores. This approach enables an evaluation of results that is independent of the variation in individual values (outliers), providing a more balanced and normalized prioritization of impacts, risks and opportunities. This methodology ensured that focus was placed on areas with the most significant influence on the Group's sustainability performance:

For positive impacts, a threshold was set at the average-based top 40% of scores, reflecting the Group's commitment to maximizing value creation and driving innovation. For opportunities a threshold was set at the average - based top 50% of scores, allowing high-potential areas where investments can generate the greatest economic, social and environmental benefits as well as Group value. Conversely, for negative impacts and risks, a more inclusive threshold of the average-based top 50% of scores was applied, to allow a wider array of potential challenges that could affect operations, stakeholder relationships and regulatory compliance through a larger portion of risks, which the Group will use to enhance resilience and reduce the likelihood of unforeseen disruptions.

##### Step 2: Stakeholder engagement in the DMA

Stakeholders play a crucial role in the Double Materiality Assessment (DMA) process. The main goal of stakeholder engagement in DMA is to incorporate their insights into corporate sustainability strategy and ESRS reporting. This is achieved by identifying and evaluating material sustainability topics through structured engagement sessions, including discussions and consultations. A key aspect of this approach involves the Stakeholder Representatives, employees who maintain close relationships with external stakeholders and facilitate the assessment process.

Stakeholders are classified into different groups based on their role and relevance:. Internal Stakeholders: Employees, shareholders and unions who shape internal decision-making and corporate culture.. Business and Market Partners: Business partners, suppliers, clients, consumers and industry peers who contribute to supply chain stability and market trends.. Financial and Investment Community: Investors, financial institutions and analysts who assess sustainability performance for investment decisions.. Regulatory and Societal Influencers: NGOs, local communities, media and policymakers who hold organizations accountable for their social and environmental impact.. Scientific and Academic Institutions: Universities and research bodies that provide insights into ESG (Environmental, Social and Governance) trends and regulations.. Environmental and Economic Systems: Nature, society and the broader economy, representing the indirect but critical stakeholders impacted by corporate sustainability actions.

Stakeholder Representatives play a crucial role in:. Conducting direct engagement with external stakeholders through structured dialogues, meetings and consultations.

· Translating stakeholder concerns into actionable insights for sustainability reporting.

· Ensuring a balanced representation of diverse stakeholder interests in the materiality assessment process.. Facilitating collaboration between different internal departments and external partners to enhance the Group's sustainability strategy.

To uphold integrity, the Group appointed a Third Party to collect insights, analyze stakeholder feedback and ensure a comprehensive and objective representation of stakeholder engagement in the double materiality scoring process.



<!-- Report Page 102 -->
#### Step 3: Validation of Impacts, Risks and Opportunities (IROs)

Before moving to the scoring phase, it was essential to validate the wording and categorization of all identified Impacts, Risks and Opportunities. This validation ensured consistency, accuracy and alignment across the Group. The process has involved cross-functional dialogue when necessary.

##### The role of the Sustainability Division in validation

The Sustainability Division was responsible for reviewing the terminology and framing of each IRO to ensure alignment with both the Group's sustainability strategy and external frameworks.

· Ensured that each impact, risk and opportunity was described in a clear and concise manner, avoiding ambiguity.. Checked that the language used reflected the nature and scope of the IRO (e.g., whether an impact was global or local, immediate, or long-term), while considering impacts throughout the entire value chain-upstream (e.g., suppliers) and downstream (e.g., customers).. Confirmed that all IROs were categorized correctly (as either risk or opportunity).

##### Internal dialogue for refinement

In cases where the wording or categorization of IROs was unclear or required further refinement, the Sustainability Division initiated an internal dialogue with relevant departments (such as Operations, Risk Management, or Human Resources). These discussions were critical to ensuring that the impacts were accurately defined and that the IRO list reflected both operational realities and strategic priorities.

The output of this phase was a validated and refined list of IROs, which was used in the subsequent scoring process. This step ensured that the foundation for the scoring phase was built on a common understanding of impacts, risks and opportunities across the organization.

#### Step 4: Scoring Impacts, Risks and Opportunities (IROs)

The scoring of impacts, risks and opportunities was conducted in alignment with the scoring system outlined in the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD) and the principles set out in ESRS 1 and ESRS 2. Once the IROs had been identified, documented and validated, the next step involved assigning scores based on their materiality. This process allowed the Group to differentiate between impact materiality (how sustainability issues affected stakeholders and the environment) and financial materiality (how sustainability issues affected the Group's value). The process integrated a double materiality perspective, ensuring a comprehensive approach to assessing sustainability matters.

#### Roles & Responsibilities 1

The governance structure for identifying and scoring impacts, risks and opportunities (IROs) within the MOTOR OIL Group is designed to ensure clarity, accountability and alignment across the entire organization. The process is segmented based on the nature and scope of the IROs, ensuring that relevant subsidiaries and data owners contribute their expertise. Additionally, the evaluation process incorporates identified key stakeholder scores per impact, which are weighted and factored into the overall score. This approach promotes a comprehensive and coordinated effort while allowing for specialization and detailed knowledge input where needed.

For the purposes of the methodology the following roles and responsibilities shall apply:. Sustainability Committee: Consisted of Board members. Responsible for the overall approval of the DMA process and the scoring results.. Sustainability Division: Is responsible for the initial scoring of the Impacts and Risks/Opportunities after having validated the IROs in step 2. It also has the general overview of the end-to-end process.. ESG Controller: Is integral part of the Sustainability division and mainly responsible for the overview of the DMA process and the regulatory compliance under CSRD.. Internal Experts: Internal expert divided into the following categories:

\- Data Owners: Are individuals that are subject matter experts selected based on their expertise in specific thematic areas and their comprehensive understanding of the companies' operations.

\- Risk Owners: Are individuals that have been assigned by the Risk Management Unit with the responsibility for identifying and assessing risks.

\- Opportunity owners: Are individuals that have been assigned by the Group Strategy Directorate with the responsibility for assessing the anticipated financial effect of opportunities.

· Stakeholders are those who can affect or be affected by the undertaking. There are two main groups of stakeholders: Affected stakeholders and key users of the sustainability statement. They participate in the process of scoring.. Third Party: Refers to external entities or organizations that assist in the process of gathering information, conducting interviews and engaging with stakeholders

Upon completion of the initial scoring by the Sustainability division, the process involved internal experts from the MOTOR OIL Group and relevant subsidiaries. If the impact was limited to a single subsidiary, the scoring responsibility rested with representatives from that specific subsidiary. The allocation of responsibility varied based on the specific sub-topic and theme of the impact, ensuring that subject matter experts with the most relevant knowledge were involved in evaluating the materiality.

In the case of financial materiality, the assessment of risks and opportunities was carried out by the respective representatives, who worked closely with two key departments:. The Risk Management Unit, which provided guidance and oversight in evaluating and scoring potential risks, ensuring a robust and systematic approach to risk identification, likelihood and financial effect assessment.. The Group Strategy General Directorate (and the relevant Business owners), which was responsible for supporting and overseeing the evaluation of opportunities, ensuring that financial projections, cost-benefit analyses and potential value creation were accurately scored.

##### Impact materiality scoring details

The impact materiality assessment focused on how sustainability matters affected external stakeholders, including communities, employees, customers and the environment. Scoring these impacts involved a thorough evaluation of three key parameters: Scale, Scope, Irreversibility and Likelihood (for potential impacts). Additionally, impacts were assessed based on their time horizon (short-term, mid-term, or longterm). These assessments were informed by insights from internal stakeholder engagement and ensured that external stakeholder concerns were reflected accurately.



<!-- Report Page 104 -->
##### Financial materiality scoring details

Financial materiality scoring focused on how sustainability-related impacts, risks and opportunities affected the Group's financial performance and value creation. Through this scoring process, the Group evaluated the likelihood of financial outcomes and considered various time horizons to determine when financial benefits or losses might materialize.

In evaluating financial materiality, key parameters were considered: Magnitude, with its underlying pillars and Likelihood of occurrence. These factors provided a comprehensive view of how sustainability matters could impact the Group's financial health.

As part of its assessment, the Group also identified how its material risks and opportunities are connected to key dependencies on natural and human capital. These dependencies were explicitly considered in evaluating the significance of each issue across the value chain.

Magnitude: The Magnitude of financial impact refers to the scale or extent of the potential financial implications that a sustainability-related activity or decision could have on the Group's overall financial health. This includes evaluating both direct and indirect financial consequences of sustainability matters, which vary widely depending on the Group's operations and market conditions.

Regulatory/Legal effect: The financial implications of regulatory compliance are vast, involving penalties, legal challenges and resource allocation. Proactive alignment enhances reputation and mitigates risks. However, non-compliance triggers audits, legal expenses and financial strain. There's potential for management prosecution, substantial lawsuits and unchanged regulatory sanctions, emphasizing the importance of strategic adherence to regulatory goals for long-term financial health and operational stability.

Operational effect: Operational efficiency, increased productivity and robust business continuity are integral to financial health. Streamlining processes cut costs, while heightened productivity optimizes resource use, impacting the bottom line. Employee loyalty and morale foster a positive work environment, curbing turnover costs and attracting talent. Effective continuity measures mitigate financial risks from disruptions, ensuring consistent operations.

Reputational effect: A strong reputation yields financial benefits through an attractive brand, fostering positive public perception. It opens doors to media and PR opportunities, enhancing visibility and market presence. Stakeholder trust, a cornerstone of financial success, is bolstered, attracting investment and partnerships. Employee advocacy, stemming from a positive reputation, cultivates a motivated workforce, driving productivity.

Strategy effect: Business/Strategic risk is a type of risk that is associated with the organization's strategic objectives and goals. It includes risks related to market competition and technological advancements. Analyzing strategic risk is important because it helps organizations identify potential threats to their business model and develop strategies to mitigate those risks.

#### Step 5: Validation from management

To ensure the robustness and strategic alignment of the identified impacts, risks and opportunities, a critical validation step was incorporated into the process, involving a review by the Sustainability Committee. This step served to verify that the assessments and scoring mechanisms were consistent with the organization's broader strategic objectives. By engaging leadership, the organization was able ensure that the findings were not only accurate but also aligned with the long-term goals, allowing for a more informed decision-making process that supports strategic priorities.



<!-- Report Page 106 -->
#### Integration of Double Materiality Assessment into the ERM

The Sustainability Division, in collaboration with an external consultant, successfully integrated the financial materiality process into the broader double materiality assessment framework. This was pivotal in enhancing the Group's understanding of how sustainability-related issues could have financial repercussions. In the past, the department had gathered a comprehensive collection of data on various sustainability impacts, but this effort was expanded by including both financial losses and benefits, which are categorized as risks or opportunities. By doing so, the Climate Change & Sustainability Unit aimed to ensure that the financial implications of these impacts were fully considered in the decision-making process.

The integration of financial materiality was not an isolated task but involved a coordinated effort with the Risk Management Unit of the Group. Their active involvement ensured that sustainability risks and opportunities were not only captured but were also aligned with the Group's overall risk management strategy. A key tool in this process was the Risk Register, which outlines the sustainability-related risks the organization faces, allowing for a more structured approach in identifying and managing these issues.

The comprehensive assessment process included both qualitative and quantitative evaluations to understand the actual or potential financial implications, whether losses or gains - that the Group might face due to its actions or impacts on the environment, society, or the economy. This dual approach provided a balanced view of risks, factoring in not just hard numbers but also the broader context and potential long-term outcomes that might not be immediately quantifiable.

To ensure each risk or opportunity was properly assessed, the Sustainability Division assigned a range of potential loss or benefit to each identified risk pillar. These pillars represented various areas of sustainability impact, such as environmental degradation, social responsibility, or economic disruption. By assigning a range of potential outcomes to each of these pillars, the Group could better gauge the significance of each risk or opportunity and prioritize its responses accordingly. This enhanced visibility into the financial ramifications of sustainability issues enabled more informed decision-making, thereby fostering a more resilient and sustainable business strategy in the face of evolving environmental and social challenges.

#### The involvement of supervisory, management and administrative in the assessment of IROs

MOTOR OIL Group ensures strong governance oversight of its impact, risk and opportunity (IRO) assessment process through the structured involvement of its administrative, management and supervisory bodies.

The Sustainability Committee which is responsible for overseeing ESG matters, plays a central role in guiding the double materiality assessment process. It reviews and validates the methodology, ensures alignment with corporate strategy and monitors how material IROs are addressed across the business. The Committee is informed of sustainability-related risks and opportunities on a regular basis and evaluates the strategic implications of the findings.

At the supervisory level, the Board of Directors holds ultimate accountability for sustainability-related decision-making. It is responsible for approving the overall business strategy, which integrates the findings of the double materiality assessment, including climate and broader ESG risks and opportunities. The BoD ensures that material IROs are considered in setting long-term objectives, in capital allocation and in major project and transaction approvals.

Additionally, the Audit Committee oversees the integrity and completeness of sustainability-related disclosures, ensuring the reliability of the assessment process and the quality of information reported in accordance with CSRD and ESRS. Finally, the Sustainability Division and the Chief Sustainability Officer coordinate the assessment on an operational level, engaging with relevant business units and subject matter experts to identify, evaluate and monitor impacts across the value chain. They ensure that the process is methodologically sound, data-driven and aligned with both regulatory requirements and internal decision-making needs.

### 1.5.2 Climate Change - Identification of Impacts, Risks and Opportunities [E1.IRO-1]

#### Climate Impacts

While MOTOR OIL Group is committed to sustainable practices, it acknowledges that the nature of its operations - particularly in Refining, Power & Gas and Fuels & Marketing - contributes to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. The refining, distribution and use of petroleum products can affect air quality and lead to broader environmental degradation. These impacts extend to various stakeholders, including nature and ecosystems, business partners, shareholders, investors, NGOs and local communities.

In addition, the Group's energy-intensive processes place considerable pressure on natural resources. Recognizing these challenges, MOTOR OIL Group continues to invest in decarbonization, energy efficiency and technological innovation, while maintaining transparent communication with stakeholders regarding environmental limitations and ongoing mitigation efforts.

#### Physical Climate Risks

MOTOR OIL Group identifies physical climate risks as a significant area of concern for its infrastructure and operations, categorizing them into acute and chronic risks. Acute risks arise from extreme weather events - such as hurricanes, floods, wildfires and heatwaves - which can cause direct physical damage, disrupt supply chains and lead to operational and financial losses. Chronic risks, on the other hand, emerge gradually due to long-term climate trends like rising temperatures, sea-level rise and prolonged droughts. These conditions can negatively impact refinery efficiency, increase energy and cooling demands, disrupt logistics and contribute to equipment corrosion, especially in coastal facilities.

Under a high-emissions scenario (RCP8.5), the Group's infrastructure is expected to experience up to 15% more downtime in high-risk locations, 8-12% productivity losses during extreme heat events and as much as 20% reduction in cooling water availability. In response, the Group has introduced adaptation measures such as closed-loop water systems and alternative sourcing strategies to maintain resilience.

In its Renewable Energy (RES) operations, physical risks vary by technology and geographical context. Solar panels and wind turbines are vulnerable to extreme weather, while temperature fluctuations can affect their performance. Water scarcity poses operational challenges for hydroelectric and cooling systems and coastal erosion and rising seas threaten offshore installations. Additionally, geophysical hazards like earthquakes and landslides can impact geothermal and hydro assets and variability in natural resources - such as inconsistent wind or solar radiation - can affect energy production and grid stability.

To mitigate these diverse risks, MOTOR OIL Group incorporates resilient infrastructure design, strategic site selection and land-use planning. The Group also utilizes climate scenario analysis to inform its strategic and project-level decision-making, ensuring that climate resilience is embedded throughout its renewable and conventional energy operations.

#### Transition Climate Risks

The global shift toward a low-carbon economy presents a broad spectrum of transition risks that have the potential to significantly impact MOTOR OIL Group's core business activities. Key challenges include regulatory developments, such as stricter emissions limits and the introduction of carbon pricing mechanisms under frameworks like the EU Green Deal and Fit for 55. In parallel, technological advancements are accelerating the competitiveness of alternative energy solutions, potentially rendering some existing fossil-based assets less viable.

At the same time, growing pressure from investors and financial institutions to prioritize sustainable portfolios, along with evolving consumer preferences and increasing societal expectations for corporate climate responsibility, are reshaping market dynamics.



Among the most critical risks is the anticipated decline in oil demand, driven by electrification and improved energy efficiency. This shift may result in lower sales volumes, reduced profit margins and underutilization of refining infrastructure. It also increases the burden of regulatory compliance and exposes the Group to reputational risks, particularly as stakeholders - including NGOs, regulators and local communities - intensify scrutiny of traditional oil and gas operations.

<!-- Report Page 108 -->
#### Climate related Opportunities

MOTOR OIL Group is proactively leveraging the energy transition by directing investments toward biofuels, green hydrogen, renewable energy infrastructure and circular economy platforms, alongside carbon capture and energy efficiency technologies. These strategic shifts are integral to the Group's transformation toward a low-carbon business model, reinforcing its commitment to achieving net-zero emissions by 2050. This realignment not only strengthens the Group's operational resilience but also ensures it remains aligned with evolving regulatory frameworks and growing stakeholder expectations around sustainability and climate performance.

With regards to the consultation with affected communities please refer to the Chapter Processes for engaging with affected communities about impacts [S3-2]. Furthermore, within the process of Double Materiality assessment (Identification of material impacts, risks and opportunities [IRO-1]), the Group has incorporated the view of local communities through the Stakeholder Representatives who are employees who maintain close relationships with external stakeholders and facilitate the assessment process. This chapter should be read in conjunction with chapter Climate Change [E]].

Finally, MOTOR OIL Group identifies and assesses climate-related risks and opportunities across short-, medium- and long-term time horizons, as part of its integrated risk management and strategic planning processes.. In the short term, key risks are primarily regulatory and technological, reflecting evolving climate legislation and the need to adapt to rapid innovation in low-carbon solutions.. In the medium term, the Group anticipates increased exposure to regulatory, technological, market and reputational risks, driven by tightening policies, shifting investor expectations and growing consumer demand for sustainable products.

· Over the long term, risks broaden further to include acute and chronic physical risks, alongside sustained regulatory, market and reputational pressures. These include the potential impacts of extreme weather events, long-term climate shifts and systemic changes in global energy demand and supply.

#### Financial Compatibility with Climate Scenarios

The Group evaluates the financial resilience of its strategy and operations under different climate scenarios, to ensure compatibility with future regulatory, market and physical risk developments as described with chapter Climate Change [E]].

### 1.5.3 Pollution - Identification of Impacts, Risks and Opportunities [E2.IRO-1]

The environmental impact of Group's operational sites has been identified, with the key entities within this sector, such as MOTOR OIL (the refinery), LPC, THERMOILEKTRIKI and VERD as well as storage and distribution facilities contribute to various forms of pollution, including air, soil and water contamination.

Due to the nature of the Group's activities, which are mainly industrial and related to oil and gas production and distribution, the pollutants emissions are unavoidable aspects. Processing and storage of fossil fuels generates substantial amounts of air pollutants beyond greenhouse gas emissions. Furthermore, industrial activities can contribute to water and soil pollution, compromising the quality of natural resources, disrupting aquatic ecosystems and posing potential health risks to local communities.

Beyond environmental implications, pollution can present financial and regulatory risks. The release of pollutants by the operational activities or accidentally can result in regulatory penalties, compliance costs and potential liabilities for environmental damage. Furthermore, the industry faces reputational risks and the potential for legal actions from NGOs and other stakeholders advocating for stricter pollution controls. Emissions of pollutants can lead to regulatory fines, health-related legal claims and increased operational costs associated with environmental compliance and remediation efforts. These risks are short-term but require proactive management to ensure regulatory adherence and mitigate potential financial consequences.

The Group's process for screening operations, business activities and assets are made through the Environmental Management System (EMS). The EMS is certified to ISO 14001 standard to the operational sites, ensuring systematic identification and control of environmental impacts.

The EMS supports the Group's sustainability objectives by:. Identifying and assessing environmental impacts throughout the production process,

· Ensuring compliance with environmental regulations and best practices and. Recording and controlling environmental performance metrics.

The EMS also aligns with European and National legislation, principally with:

· Directive 2010/75/EU (IED), focusing on integrated air, water and soil pollution prevention.. Directive 2015/2193/EU on the limitation of emissions of certain pollutants into the air from medium combustion plants.. Regulations on Best Available Techniques implementation. Directive 2011/92/EU on the assessment of the effects of certain public and private projects on the environment during construction and operation

The pollution related topics that were included in the materiality assessment can be found below:

Air Pollution: The processing of fossil fuels emits significant levels of pollutants - excluding greenhouse gases - such as sulfur oxides (SOx), nitrogen oxides (NOx), volatile organic compounds (VOCs) and particulate matter (PM). These emissions contribute to atmospheric degradation, smog formation, acid rain and adverse health effects on surrounding communities.

Wastewater and Surface Water Pollution: The discharge of process water and cooling water, if not properly treated, can introduce pollutants into surface water bodies, affecting aquatic biodiversity and water quality.

In the Circular Economy and Alternative Fuels sector, the impacts include the following issues:

Clogged Sewers: When used cooking oils are poured down drains, they can solidify and cause blockages



in the sewer systems. This can lead to costly repairs and maintenance, as well as potential overflow of sewage, which poses health risks to communities.

Water Contamination: Improper disposal of cooking oils can lead to contamination of water bodies. Oils can create a film on the surface of water, reducing oxygen levels and harming aquatic life. This contamination can also affect drinking water sources, posing risks to human health.

Air Pollution: When cooking oils are not disposed of properly and are instead burned or incinerated, they can release harmful pollutants into the air. These pollutants contribute to air quality degradation and can have adverse effects on respiratory health.

Pollution-related risks are assessed from both operational and financial perspectives. Similarly, the risks associated with pollution and included in the materiality assessment are the following:

Water Contamination from Oil Spills: Accidental spills or leaks into water bodies may result in extensive environmental damage and significant financial liabilities. These include remediation costs, potential penalties from regulatory authorities, operational disruptions and reputational harm. Legal actions by affected communities, NGOs, or environmental bodies may also arise.

Regulatory and Legal Risk: The handling of hazardous substances and the generation of waste streams expose the Group to regulatory risks. Stricter environmental regulations or non-compliance may lead to fines, operational restrictions, or permit withdrawals. There is also exposure to litigation related to health impacts and environmental degradation.

Operational and Compliance Costs: Meeting increasingly stringent pollution control standards necessitates ongoing investment in advanced treatment technologies, emissions monitoring systems and staff training. These result in elevated capital and operational expenditures.

With regards to the consultation with affected communities please refer to the Chapter Processes for engaging with affected communities about impacts [S3-2].

<!-- Report Page 110 -->
### 1.5.4 Water and Marine Resources - Identification of Impacts, Risks and Opportunities [E3.IRO-1]

Recognizing the importance of water as a shared and finite resource, MOTOR OIL Group prioritizes efficient water usage and wastewater management across its industrial activities. The Group monitors water consumption, reuses water where feasible and ensures that effluent discharges meet environmental standards, thereby protecting natural water systems. Investments in closed-loop systems and water treatment technologies support the goal of reducing the water footprint while maintaining operational excellence. The water-related topics that were included in the materiality assessment are analyzed below and include water usage and desalination, among others.

To reduce its water consumption, MOTOR OIL Group consistently implements measures to efficiently manage water use, especially at the refinery. To this extent, the refinery operates a desalinization unit which allows it to cover 100% of its water needs by sea water. This initiative is crucial in conserving freshwater resources and reducing the strain on local water supplies.

Furthermore, the refinery processes acidic water generated in different phases of the production process, recycling a large portion of the flow where possible. This recycling effort is essential in minimizing waste and reducing the environmental impact of industrial operations. By treating and reusing acidic water, the refinery significantly lowers the volume of wastewater released into the environment, thereby protecting aquatic ecosystems and maintaining water quality.

As a result of these measures, impacts to water and the natural environment are eliminated to the lowest degree possible. Specifically, the following benefits are achieved:. Conservation of Freshwater Resources: By relying entirely on desalinated sea water, the refinery avoids the use of freshwater sources, which are often limited and vital for local communities and ecosystems. This helps in preserving freshwater for essential uses such as drinking water and agriculture.. Protection of Marine Ecosystems: The desalination process ensures that the water used in the refinery does not negatively affect marine life. Proper management and treatment of sea water prevent contamination and disruption of marine habitats.. Reduction of Wastewater Pollution: Recycling acidic water within the refinery reduces the amount of polluted water discharged into the environment. This helps in maintaining the health of nearby water bodies and prevents harmful substances from entering the natural water cycle.

As a result of Group's efforts to eliminate the impact of its operations on the area's natural ecosystems, 100% of the water used in various parts of the refinery originates from processed sea water at its desalination units. This comprehensive approach to water management demonstrates MOTOR OIL Group's commitment to sustainability and environmental stewardship.

Through these initiatives, MOTOR OIL Group not only ensures efficient water use but also actively contributes to the protection and preservation of marine resources. Group's dedication to sustainable practices reflects its responsibility towards the environment and its goal of fostering a healthier and more sustainable future for all.

MOTOR OIL's approach to water management is grounded in operational excellence and environmental compliance. The refinery, which accounts for more than 86% of total water withdrawals, relies solely on seawater through desalination, significantly limiting the use of freshwater sources. All industrial sites are equipped with Wastewater Treatment Plants (WWTPs) and no untreated discharges are released into freshwater or groundwater. Daily monitoring ensures water quality, with tertiary treatment applied at key facilities such as MOTOR OIL refinery. Water-related activities are governed under ISO 14001-certified Environmental Management Systems.

Water is also treated as a climate-adjacent risk and relevant planning is integrated into the Group's broader climate adaptation and emergency preparedness strategy. For example, sites located in flood-prone areas (as defined under the EU Floods Directive) have contingency plans in place. Marine risks are addressed through a Facility Contingency Plan aligned with international marine protection standards, covering pollution prevention and spill response. Biodiversity and water risk management are closely interconnected, particularly in coastal and port operations, where wastewater handling and spill mitigation protocols are specifically designed to protect marine ecosystems.

Water is a critical resource across several of MOTOR OIL Group's core business activities, with each segment facing distinct water-related risks due to the nature of its operations.

This high dependency on water resources presents several material risks. Potential regulatory restrictions on water withdrawals or discharge quality may require costly operational adjustments or investments in advanced treatment technologies. Additionally, any failure in desalination infrastructure or deterioration in seawater quality due to pollution or climate-related impacts could disrupt operations.

While the direct water footprint in retail operations is relatively low, environmental risks remain. These include the potential for localized water pollution (e.g., spills or runoff at fuel stations), non-compliance with wastewater handling regulations and reputational risks if environmental safeguards are not properly enforced. Increasing consumer expectations for eco-efficient services - such as water-saving car wash systems - highlight the importance of continuous environmental performance across the retail network.

Power generation and gas infrastructure operations also involve water use, particularly in cooling and process applications. In this segment, risks are linked to physical water scarcity, regulatory compliance for discharge quality and potential temperature-based effluent restrictions under environmental permits.



Climate variability may increase the volatility of water availability or quality, requiring investment in resilient technologies such as closed-loop systems or air-cooling alternatives. In natural gas operations, occasional use of water in equipment cleaning and pressure systems requires proper wastewater disposal and monitoring.

Additionally, the Group operates in the environmental services sector, where water plays a central role in waste treatment processes. Risks in this segment relate to the quality of treated effluents, compliance with stringent discharge standards and the management of stormwater runoff from storage or landfill sites. Failures in water quality management could result in fines, reputational damage.

Finally, as the Group expands into biofuels and low-carbon alternatives (e.g., biodiesel, e-methanol, Blue Med initiatives), new water-related risks emerge. These include the water intensity of certain feedstocks, risks of unsustainable agricultural practices and discharge compliance from chemical processing.

The Group is also aware of potential community concerns around large-scale water use, particularly in regions facing seasonal scarcity.

With regards to the consultation with affected communities please refer to the Chapter Processes for engaging with affected communities about impacts [S3-2].

Furthermore, within the process of Double Materiality assessment (Identification of material impacts, risks and opportunities [IRO-1]), the Group has incorporated the view of local communities through the Stakeholder Representatives who are employees who maintain close relationships with external stakeholders and facilitate the assessment process.

### 1.5.5 Biodiversity and Ecosystems - Identification of Impacts, Risks and Opportunities [E4.IRO-1]

MOTOR OIL Group identified its material impacts, risks and opportunities related to biodiversity and ecosystem matters through the double materiality process. In the double materiality process, the Group evaluated both the impact of its operations on stakeholders and the broader environment, as well as the risks and opportunities emerging from external trends, including regulatory, market and societal developments. To support this, the Group employs a robust set of criteria for materiality assessment, encompassing factors such as geographic location, operational activities and industry-specific dynamics. The geographical context of operations, the nature of refinery activities and sector-specific challenges are critical elements in identifying material risks and opportunities. The topics that were included in the materiality assessment were related to Land-use change, Species population size and Land degradation. It should be noticed that the Group has not taken into consideration transition and physical risks, or systemic risks related to biodiversity and ecosystems.

Biodiversity preservation is embedded into environmental risk assessments, especially in areas adjacent to sensitive ecosystems. The Group undertakes proactive measures to mitigate habitat disruption and supports initiatives that promote ecosystem restoration and environmental stewardship.

MOTOR OIL Group integrates its refinery and commercial facilities into the marine environment with careful planning to prevent and respond to pollution incidents. No parts of the refinery about natural habitats or protected areas, minimizing direct harm to sensitive ecosystems.

CORAL & AVIN conduct regular spill response drills involving local authorities and certified contractors, equipped with emergency vans to address spills promptly. AVIN OIL identifies and manages environmental risks, such as degradation at its Agioi Theodoroi loading station, following strict operational rules and preventive plans.

All projects undertaken by MORE in 2023 were licensed according to Greek legislation, ensuring environmental approval for new constructions and addressing biodiversity impacts through assessments.

OFC conducts precise monitoring of groundwater, effluent, surface water, vapor and soil to detect and address environmental issues. MOTOR OIL Group's commitment to biodiversity includes strategic facility placement, detailed emergency plans, risk identification and management, compliance with legislation and comprehensive environmental monitoring. These initiatives protect biodiversity and promote sustainable practices, reflecting the group's dedication to a healthier and more sustainable future.

With regards to the consultation with affected communities please refer to the Chapter Processes for engaging with affected communities about impacts [S3-2]. Furthermore, within the process of Double Materiality assessment (Identification of material impacts, risks and opportunities [IRO-1]), the Group has incorporated the view of local communities through the Stakeholder Representatives who are employees who maintain close relationships with external stakeholders and facilitate the assessment process.

### 1.5.6 Resource Use and Circular Economy - Identification of Impacts, Risks and Opportunities [E5.IRO-1]

The Group actively promotes waste minimization, reuse and recycling across its operations by adopting circular economy principles. MOTOR OIL Group reduces waste generation through process optimization, responsible consumption and sustainable packaging initiatives. Non-recyclable and hazardous waste are managed in compliance with environmental regulations, ensuring safe disposal and minimal environmental impact. The Group also explores innovative methods to extend product life cycles and improve resource efficiency throughout the value chain.

Aiming to minimize its environmental impacts, MOTOR OIL Group has developed and implemented an Environmental Policy and Management System while also employing Best Available Techniques. Specifically, for the refinery, there is an extensive approach towards waste management detailed in the relevant procedure, which forms part of the Environmental Management System. The purpose of this procedure is:. Recording of Solid Waste: Comprehensive documentation of all solid waste produced during the operation of the refinery.. Classification of Solid Waste: Categorizing solid waste according to its physico-chemical composition and hazardous properties.. Management Presentation: Outlining the overall management of waste in accordance with applicable environmental terms governing the operation of the refinery and current legislation.

Inadequate waste management across MOTOR OIL Group's refinery, fuel storage and production operations pose a significant environmental and operational risk. Failure to effectively contain and treat hazardous and non-hazardous waste can lead to soil and groundwater contamination, resulting in biodiversity loss, water scarcity and long-term environmental degradation. These impacts may trigger costly remediation efforts, regulatory penalties and reputational damage.

RES activities, while essential for clean energy production, contribute to waste generation through the gradual deterioration of infrastructure. Solar panels, wind turbine blades and other renewable energy components have a limited lifespan, requiring systematic decommissioning and disposal strategies. Additionally, the widespread adoption of energy storage solutions introduces new challenges, particularly concerning the handling of end-of-life batteries. Without effective waste management frameworks, the accumulation of decommissioned renewable energy equipment could undermine the sustainability benefits of clean energy technologies.



Despite these challenges, waste repurpose offers an opportunity to the Group to transform industrial by-products and discarded materials into valuable resources.

Within the MOTOR OIL Group, companies specializing in circular economy play a key role in reducing landfill dependency and enhancing operational sustainability. By recovering and reusing materials, MOTOR OIL's subsidiaries such as VERD, PRASINO LADI, LPC, THALIS can create a closed-loop system that minimizes waste while improving economic efficiency. This approach aligns with global sustainability trends, supporting to meet compliance with evolving environmental regulations and stakeholder expectations.

Waste management companies further contribute to sustainability by implementing advancedrecycling programs, promoting responsible waste management practices and reducing landfill disposal. By collaborating with industries, policymakers and technology providers, these companies help develop solutions that balance operational efficiency with environmental responsibility. As industries transition toward more sustainable business models, integrating waste upcycling into business strategies will be essential in mitigating environmental risks and reinforcing corporate sustainability commitments.

#### Risks

The risk of hazardous waste contamination presents immediate and substantial financial liabilities for companies involved in operations that generate hazardous materials - such as MOTOR OIL Group's refining, fuel storage and distribution activities. Cleanup costs can escalate quickly due to the complexity and scale of remediation efforts required, making them one of the most significant financial concerns in the sector. Additionally, the refining industry operates under strict and evolving environmental regulations, where non-compliance may lead to severe regulatory penalties. Inadequate waste management not only risks regulatory breaches but also exposes the Group to potential litigation from affected communities, amplifying financial and reputational risks.

In the renewable energy sector where MORE operates, the growing waste from decommissioned solar panels, wind turbine blades and energy storage systems present rising financial risks. Disposal costs are significant and compounded by evolving waste regulations. These components often contain hazardous materials and improper handling can lead to soil and water contamination, resulting in fines, legal action and costly remediation.

#### Opportunities

The accelerating shift toward circular economy practices and stricter environmental regulations has created a significant strategic opportunity for MOTOR OIL Group to diversify and future-proof its operations. As industries and governments intensify efforts to reduce waste, recover resources and lower emissions, the demand for sustainable waste management solutions has surged - particularly in sectors such as energy, mobility and manufacturing. Recognizing this momentum, MOTOR OIL Group has positioned itself as a key player in this transition by developing one of the most integrated circular economy platforms in Greece.

With regards to the consultation with affected communities please refer to the Chapter Processes for engaging with affected communities about impacts [$3-2]. Furthermore, within the process of Double Materiality assessment (Identification of material impacts, risks and opportunities [IRO-1]), the Group has incorporated the view of local communities through the Stakeholder Representatives who are employees who maintain close relationships with external stakeholders and facilitate the assessment process.

<!-- Report Page 114 -->
### 1.5.7 Business Conduct - Identification of Impacts, Risks and Opportunities [G1.IRO-1]

The Group identified its material impacts, risks and opportunities related to business conduct matters through a robust double materiality process. This approach ensured that the Group not only evaluated the financial implications of its activities but also considered the environmental, social and governance (ESG) factors that may influence its long-term value creation.

In the double materiality process, the Group considered both the impact of its operations on stakeholders and the broader environment, as well as the risks and opportunities arising from external trends, including regulatory, market and societal shifts. To achieve this, the Group uses a comprehensive set of criteria when assessing materiality, including location, activity, sector and the structure of its transactions.

The geographical context of operations, the nature of refinery activities, sector-specific challenges and the structure of transactions all play pivotal roles in identifying material risks and opportunities. The Group evaluates the environmental and social conditions of the areas in which it operates, taking into account local regulations, community impacts and risks related to climate change or other regional factors. The refinery's operations are assessed for their potential effects on business conduct, including production processes, energy consumption, waste management and social or ethical implications such as worker safety and environmental degradation. Sector-specific risks, such as compliance with environmental standards, supply chain transparency and resource management, are also considered. Furthermore, the structure of transactions like mergers, acquisitions, or partnerships is carefully examined to identify any risks or opportunities that could impact governance, ethical practices and the integration of sustainability criteria into corporate strategies.

Throughout the double materiality process, the Group actively engages with as many as possible affected stakeholders to ensure that their perspectives are considered in the decision-making process. This multistakeholder approach ensures that material impacts, risks and opportunities are comprehensively identified, allowing the Group to make informed, responsible decisions that align with both its business objectives and broader sustainability goals.

## 2 EU Taxonomy





## 2 EU TAXONOMY

<!-- Report Page 126 -->
### 2.1 Introduction to the Regulation (EU) 2020/852

In 2020, the European Parliament adopted the EU Taxonomy Regulation, creating an EU-wide framework that helps investors and businesses determine whether certain economic activities are environmentally sustainable. Aligned with the European Union's strategic vision of promoting resilient and sustainable economies and in accordance with the climate and energy targets for 2030 and the objectives of the European Green Deal, the European Commission introduced a classification system of sustainable activities under the Taxonomy Regulation. On 4 June 2021, the European Commission introduced the Commission Delegated Regulation (EU) 2021/2139, which defines the technical screening criteria for climate change mitigation and adaptation objectives. Later, on 6 July 2021, the Commission also adopted the Commission Delegated Regulation (EU) 2021/2178 (the "Disclosures Delegated Act"), which complements Article 8 of the Taxonomy Regulation and outlines the content, methodology and format for disclosing information on the proportion of environmentally sustainable activities in the operations, investments and lending activities of both financial and non-financial undertakings. On 15 July 2022, a Complementary Climate Delegated Act was released in the Official Journal of the EU, including, under strict conditions, specific nuclear and gas energy activities in the list of economic activities covered by the EU Taxonomy.

Within the EU Taxonomy, the following six environmental objectives are identified:. Climate change mitigation. Climate change adaptation. Sustainable use and protection of water and marine resources. Pollution prevention and control

· Transition to a circular economy. Protection and restoration of biodiversity and ecosystems.

Climate change mitigation and climate change adaptation objectives were set out in the Climate Delegated Act and have been applied since 2021, while the remaining four objectives came into force in June 2023 under the Environmental Delegated Act and are effective from this reporting year onwards.

For each one of these objectives, the Delegated Acts define which activities are eligible and/or aligned. For an economic activity to be considered eligible it is adequate to be outlined in the Delegated Act for the environmental objective to which substantially contributes. Alternatively, for an economic activity to be classified as aligned, it shall meet the following criteria:. Meet the technical screening criteria defined for per activity.. Do no significant harm to any of the remaining objectives.

· Comply with the minimum social safeguards' requirements.

According to the Regulation, companies are required to disclose the percentage of their eligible economic activities and the extent to which they are aligned with the above criteria through the key performance indicators defined by the Taxonomy Regulation (turnover, capital expenditure, operational expenditure).

### 2.2 Application of the Taxonomy Regulation to MOTOR OIL Group

To ensure full compliance with the Taxonomy Regulation, MOTOR OIL Hellas Group conducted a comprehensive evaluation of both eligibility and alignment by thoroughly assessing its economic activities against the six environmental objectives outlined in the Climate Delegated Act and the Environmental Delegated Act. Based on these assessments, the Group calculated the turnover, capital expenditure (CapEx) and operational expenditure (OpEx) associated with each identified eligible and aligned economic activity.

The scope of this analysis encompasses all consolidated subsidiaries of MOTOR OIL Group, as reported in the financial statement, in line with Regulation 2020/852 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 18 June 2020.

This year's EU Taxonomy disclosures also include data collected from the Group's subsidiaries:. Anemos Group. Avin Oil Group

· Associate Companies, on a voluntary basis.. Coral Gas Group. Coral Group. LPC Group. Motor Oil Renewable Energy (MORE) Group. Motor Oil Group. Ventus Group

For the purposes of these disclosures, the above entities will hereinafter be referred to as MOTOR OIL Group, or the Group. This report presents both eligible and non-eligible activities, as well as aligned and non-aligned ones, for the reporting period ending on December 31, 2024.

The Group's activities substantially contribute to the following environmental objectives:. Climate change mitigation (CCM). Transition to circular economy (CE). Pollution prevention and control (PPC)

<!-- Report Page 128 -->
· Sustainable use and protection of water and marine resources (WTR)

### 2.2.1 Eligibility assessment

The Group's business activities were evaluated for eligibility based on the Taxonomy Regulation, including Delegated Regulation EU 2021/2139 ("Climate Delegated Act," as amended), Delegated Regulation EU 2022/1214 ("Complementary Climate Delegated Act") and Delegated Regulation EU 2023/2486 ("Environmental Delegated Act"). To identify eligible activities across all six environmental objectives, the assessment structured the Group's business activities according to their nature and associated NACE codes.

### 2.2.2 Alignment assessment

#### Technical Screening Criteria (TSC)

In the next phase, each eligible activity from the Group's operations was assessed against the relevant substantial contribution criteria for Climate Change Mitigation, Circular Economy, Pollution Prevention and control and Water objectives, as specified in the Climate Delegated Act, the Environmental Delegated Act and their amendments. A thorough evaluation of the technical screening criteria confirmed that 17 out of 26, (approximately 65%) activities meet the TSC requirements.

#### Do No Significant Harm (DNSH)

For eligible activities that meet their respective TSC, as identified in the previous phase, the Group has applied the guidance outlined in Article 17 of the Taxonomy Regulation, along with the Climate Delegated Act and Environmental Delegated Act, to evaluate their compliance with the relevant DNSH criteria. Please refer to the 'Emblematic Activities' section for a detailed overview of the evaluation results of key economic activities that meet their respective DNSH criteria. The assessment revealed that 100% of the activities reviewed against the relevant TSC comply with the DNSH criteria.

#### Minimum Social Safeguards (MSS)

In addition to environmental criteria, MOTOR OIL Group also assures full compliance with the minimum social safeguards outlined in Article 18 of the Regulation2. These safeguards require adherence to the OECD Guidelines for Multinational Enterprises and the United Nations Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights. They also encompass the principles and rights established in the eight fundamental conventions of the International Labour Organization (ILO) and the International Charter of Human Rights. The Group has carefully reviewed all applicable requirements and has implemented the necessary procedures and policies to align with these standards.

It is important to highlight that the Group has already been reporting under the EU Taxonomy framework and according to the latest updates of the Disclosures Delegated Act3, it remains committed to advancing transparency and sustainability. In line with this commitment, MOTOR OIL Group has once again opted to voluntarily assess and disclose the alignment of these newly identified activities. Additionally, the Group has extended its reporting scope to include associate companies on a voluntary basis, further enhancing the comprehensiveness of its disclosures. This proactive approach reinforces its dedication to sustainable business practices, responsible investment and continuous improvement in environmental performance.

### 2.3 Emblematic Activities

#### 2.3.1 Revenue generating economic activities in renewable energy sources

##### 4.1 - Electricity generation using solar photovoltaic technology

Toreduce its reliance on fossil fuels for energy generation, the Group has taken a leading role in advancing research and implementing innovative projects in renewable energy. Through its commitment to sustainability, the Group has successfully developed 52 solar photovoltaic parks, collectively boasting a total capacity of 50 MW.

These efforts represent a significant step toward transitioning to cleaner, more sustainable energy sources. MOTOR OIL Group has approximately 0.03% aligned turnover, 9% aligned capex and 0.38% aligned opex.

Since the activity has not been classified as a CCA enabling activity, compliance with the DNSH criteria for CCM was not assessed. Moreover, Water & Pollution Prevention DNSH criteria were not applicable.

##### DNSH to Climate Change Adaptation

The activity complies with all generic criteria for DNSH to climate change adaptation. For more details, please refer to the 'General Disclosures - ESRS 2' and to 'ESRS E1 - Climate change' sections, specifically the TCFD analysis, where you can review the physical climate risks material to the activity, as well as the results of the comprehensive climate risk and vulnerability assessment.

##### DNSH to Transition to a Circular Economy

The activity assesses availability of and, where feasible, uses equipment and components of high durability and recyclability and that are easy to dismantle and refurbish.

##### DNSH to Biodiversity

The Group has conducted the relevant Environmental Impact Assessments (EIAs) for each wind park in accordance with Directive 2011/92/EU. All required mitigation and compensation measures to protect the environment have been implemented. Specifically, for sites or operations located in or near biodiversitysensitive areas - such as the Natura 2000 network of protected areas, UNESCO World Heritage sites, Key Biodiversity Areas and other protected areas - an appropriate assessment was carried out. Based on its conclusions, no additional mitigation measures were deemed necessary, as such the activity complies with the criteria set out in Appendix D to this Annex.

###### 4.3 - Electricity generation from wind power

MORE is a leading force in the field of RES, one of the largest RES producers in Greece and one of the most important in Southeastern Europe. MORE is an important pillar of MOTOR OIL Group's energy transition plan, as it generates renewable electricity from wind power. Specifically, the company operates a modern portfolio of 839 MW installed capacity, 94% of which is wind-based power generation units.

MOTOR OIL Group has achieved approximately 1% aligned turnover, 10% aligned capex and 14.77% aligned opex, reflecting a strong commitment to aligning its financial activities with its Transition Plan.

Since the activity has not been classified as a CCA enabling activity, compliance with the DNSH criteria for climate change mitigation (CCM) was not evaluated. Additionally, the Pollution Prevention DNSH criteria were not applicable.



<!-- Report Page 134 -->
##### DNSH to Climate Change Adaptation

The activity complies with the criteria set out in Appendix A of Annex I to Climate Delegated Act, which specifies the generic criteria for DNSH to climate change adaptation. For additional complementary information on the Group's climate risk and vulnerability assessment, including climate adaptation plans, refer to 'General Disclosures - ESRS 2' and to 'ESRS E1 - Climate change' sections.

##### DNSH to Water

The Group operates exclusively onshore wind and as such, its activities do not impede the achievement of good environmental status as outlined in Directive 2008/56/EC of the European Parliament and of the Council. This Directive requires that the appropriate measures are taken to prevent or mitigate impacts in relation to that Directive's Descriptor 11 (Noise/Energy), with which the Group is compliant.

##### DNSH to Transition to a Circular Economy

For this activity, MOTOR OIL Group assesses availability of and, where feasible, used equipment and components of high durability and recyclability and that are easy to dismantle and refurbish.

##### DNSH to Biodiversity

MOTOR OIL Group has performed an Environmental Impact Assessment in accordance with Directive 2011/92/EU for all onshore wind facilities. All necessary mitigation and compensation measures for protecting the surrounding environment were implemented. It is also worth mentioning that the Group has no onshore wind sites located in or near biodiversity-sensitive areas.

##### 4.13 Manufacture of biogas and biofuels for use in transport and of bioliquids

The circular economy sector and renewable alternative fuels is an important aspect in the overall business strategy of MOTOR OIL Group, which is expanding its activities in this sector with new investments, partnerships and acquisitions. Over the last two years, significant investments have been made in both of these sectors.

The Group's aligned figures are approximately 0.1% for turnover, 0.2% for capex and 0.66% for opex.

As the activity has not been classified as a CCA enabling activity, compliance with the DNSH criteria for climate change mitigation (CCM) was not assessed. Furthermore, the Circular Economy DNSH criteria were not applicable.

##### DNSH to Climate Change Adaptation

The activity meets the criteria outlined in Appendix A of Annex I to the Climate Delegated Act, which defines the generic DNSH criteria for climate change adaptation. For further details on the Group's climate risk and vulnerability assessment, as well as climate adaptation plans, please refer to the 'General Disclosures - ESRS 2' and 'ESRS E1 - Climate change' sections.

##### DNSH to Water

The Group actively identifies and addresses environmental degradation risks, particularly those related to preserving water quality and preventing water stress, with the goal of achieving good water status and ecological potential. To manage these risks, a water-use and protection management plan is developed for any potentially affected water bodies, in consultation with relevant stakeholders. Additionally, the Group ensures that an Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) is conducted in accordance with Directive 2011/92/EU to evaluate and mitigate any potential environmental impacts.

##### DNSH to Pollution Prevention and Control

MOTOR OIL Group applies a gas-tight cover on the digestate storage for biogas production and they also follow the BATs, as such no significant cross-media effects occurred.

##### DNSH to Biodiversity

MOTOR OIL Group has conducted an Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) for all manufacturing facilities of biogas and biofuels for transport, as well as bioliquids, in accordance with Directive 2011/92/ EU. All necessary mitigation and compensation measures to protect the surrounding environment have been fully implemented. Notably, all relevant facilities are located in a designated industrial zone, far from biodiversity-sensitive areas.

##### 4.14 Transmission and distribution networks for renewable and low-carbon gases

CORAL GAS is constantly investing in innovation and safety. It provides all its packaged products with a special safety valve offering a set of innovative products that raise the safety level of LPG consumers in Greece.

The Group's aligned ratios stand at approximately 1% for turnover, 2% for capex and 0.28% for opex.

Since the activity is not classified as CCA enabling, its compliance with the DNSH criteria for climate change mitigation (CCM) was not reviewed. Similarly, the Circular Economy DNSH criteria were not applicable.

##### DNSH to Climate Change Adaptation

The activity complies with the requirements set out in Appendix A of Annex I to the Climate Delegated Act, which establishes the generic DNSH criteria for climate change adaptation. For more information on the Group's climate risk and vulnerability assessment, along with its climate adaptation plans, please refer to the 'General Disclosures - ESRS 2' and 'ESRS E1 - Climate Change' sections.

##### DNSH to Water

The Group mitigates environmental risks by safeguarding water quality and preventing water stress to achieve good water status. It develops a water management plan for affected areas in consultation with stakeholders and conducts Environmental Impact Assessments (EIAs) per Directive 2011/92/EU.

##### DNSH to Pollution Prevention and Control

All fans, compressors, pumps and other equipment used in transmission and distribution networks for renewable and low-carbon gases comply with Directive 2009/125/EC, adhering to top-class energy label requirements and relevant implementing regulations. These systems represent the best available technology.

##### DNSH to Biodiversity

MOTOR OIL Group has conducted an Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) for all facilities related to the transmission and distribution of renewable and low-carbon gases, in accordance with Directive 2011/92/EU. The required mitigation and compensation measures to protect the environment have been implemented.



<!-- Report Page 136 -->
### 2.4 Accounting policy

The MOTOR OIL Group's consolidated financial statements for the 2024 financial year have been prepared in accordance with International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS). The following sections present details on turnover, CapEx and OpEx for the Group's relevant subsidiaries, as previously introduced in this report. By translating the Group's environmentally friendly initiatives and outcomes into financial metrics - turnover, CapEx and OpEx - sinvestors and financial institutions gain a clearer perspective for making informed and strategic decisions. The methodology for calculating eligibility and alignment KPIs is outlined below:. Turnover reflects the share of net turnover generated from products or services that are taxonomyeligible and/or aligned. The Turnover KPI provides a static snapshot of the Group's contribution to environmental objectives.. CapEx represents the portion of capital expenditure related to activities that are already taxonomyeligible and/or aligned or are part of a credible plan to achieve taxonomy alignment. This KPI offers a dynamic and forward-looking perspective on the Group's strategy for transforming its business activities.. OpEx denotes the share of operating expenditure linked to taxonomy-eligible and/or aligned activities or the CapEx plan. It includes direct non-capitalized costs for research and development, renovation measures, short-term leases and maintenance, as well as other direct expenses necessary for the day-to-day servicing of property, plant and equipment to ensure their continued and effective use.

### 2.4.1 Avoiding double counting

MOTOR OIL Group ensures that double counting and intragroup effects are eliminated in the EU Taxonomy assessment through the structured approach of its financial statements and the detailed tagging of CapEx and OpEx accounts. This meticulous methodology enhances transparency, ensuring the accurate classification of financial data and maintaining consistency across the reporting period. The accounting policy has not changed during the reporting period.

To uphold regulatory compliance and best practices, the Group remains committed to closely monitoring any updates to the EU Taxonomy. It continuously refines its methodologies for capturing and reporting relevant information, integrating any modifications into its financial framework.

Furthermore, MOTOR OIL Group actively engages in internal reviews and audits to validate the accuracy of its financial and sustainability disclosures. Collaboration across departments ensures that taxonomy-related financial data is correctly allocated and aligned with regulatory requirements. This internal oversight strengthens the integrity of the Group's reporting process and supports its broader commitment to responsible and transparent corporate governance.

### 2.4.2 Changes in disclosures compared with the previous reporting period

With the adoption of the Environmental Delegated Act in 2023, the EU Taxonomy has been expanded to cover additional economic activities. The Group has evaluated several of these activities and determined their relevance for inclusion in its disclosures starting from the 2024 fiscal year. As presented in the 'EU Taxonomy KPIs' section and more specifically in the dedicated tables during the reporting period the Group identified additional eligible economic activities.

The eligibility and alignment percentages for turnover, CapEx and OpEx have all increased, reflecting the Group's expanded scope and continued commitment to sustainability.

The rise in the Group's aligned turnover ratio by 12% and OpEx ratio by approximately 6.95% is driven by the inclusion of additional subsidiaries and activities under the EU Taxonomy framework. Similarly, CapEx alignment ratio has also grown by circa 19%, supported by targeted investments in sustainable projects and infrastructure improvements. These increases demonstrate the Group's strategic focus on enhancing its sustainability performance through both operational expenditures and long-term capital investments.

MOTOR OIL Group's ongoing commitment to environmental sustainability is reflected in the improved alignment ratios in this year's report, further reinforcing its dedication to integrating sustainable practices into its financial and operational framework.

### 2.8 EU Taxonomy Appendix

The key performance indicators (KPIs) for eligible (aligned and non-aligned) and non-eligible economic activities of Motor Oil Hellas Group have been calculated in accordance with the Accounting Policy outlined in the Disclosure Delegated Act 2021/2178. The following sections outline the methodology used to determine the KPIs for Turnover, Capital Expenditure (CapEx) and Operating Expenditure (OpEx).

#### Turnover KPI

The Turnover KPI represents the proportion of net turnover derived from products or services, including intangibles, that are associated with Taxonomy-aligned economic activities. It is calculated by dividing the net turnover related to these activities (numerator) by the total net turnover (denominator), as defined in Article 2, point (5) of Directive 2013/34/EU. Turnover is recognized in accordance with International Accounting Standard (IAS) 1, paragraph 82(a), as adopted by Commission Regulation (EC) No 1126/2008.

The KPI calculation excludes turnover derived from activities adapted to climate change under Article 11(1), point (a) of Regulation (EU) 2020/852 and Annex II of Delegated Regulation (EU) 2021/2139, unless they qualify as enabling activities under Article 11(1), point (b) of the Regulation or are themselves Taxonomy-eligible and aligned. For activities contributing to the environmental objective of climate change adaptation, the numerator includes turnover generated by enabling activities.

#### Capital Expenditure (CapEx) KPI

The CapEx KPI is calculated as the proportion of capital expenditure associated with Taxonomy-aligned activities, in accordance with Article 8(2), point (b) of Regulation (EU) 2020/852. The KPI is determined by dividing the numerator by the denominator, as specified in Annex I, sections 1.1.2.1 and 1.1.2.2 of Delegated Regulation (EU) 2021/2178, as amended.

##### Denominator

The denominator encompasses all capital investments made during the financial year, reflecting the Group's expenditures on tangible and intangible assets that contribute to long-term value creation. These additions are considered before applying depreciation, amortization, or any re-measurements resulting from asset revaluations or impairments. The denominator also includes capital expenditures arising from business combinations, ensuring a comprehensive view of the Group's investment activities.

##### Numerator

The numerator consists of the portion of capital expenditure included in the denominator that meets one of the following criteria:. Directly associated with Taxonomy-aligned economic activities.. Part of a CapEx plan to expand Taxonomy-aligned economic activities or transition Taxonomyeligible activities to alignment, in compliance with the conditions set in the Regulation.. Linked to the purchase of output from Taxonomy-aligned economic activities.

The numerator also specifies CapEx allocations contributing to each environmental objective. When CapEx contributes to both climate change mitigation and adaptation, but no clear separation can be made, it is included under the mitigation objective.

##### Operating Expenditure (OpEx) KPI

The OpEx KPI measures the proportion of operating expenditures associated with Taxonomy-aligned activities, as outlined in Article 8(2), point (b) of Regulation (EU) 2020/852. It is calculated by dividing the numerator by the denominator, as specified in Annex I, sections 1.1.3.1 and 1.1.3.2 of Delegated Regulation (EU) 2021/2178, as amended.

###### Denominator

The denominator encompasses direct non-capitalized costs that are essential for maintaining and enhancing the operational efficiency and sustainability of the Group's assets. These costs are incurred as part of routine business activities and are not capitalized under financial accounting principles. The key categories included in the denominator are:. Research and development (R&D): Expenses, which include R&D costs that are not capitalized should be counted as eligible OpEx. If they are capitalized, they should be included as eligible CapEx.. Repair and maintenance: It includes any maintenance and repair costs carried out to ensure the continued and effective functioning of the asset.. Short term leases: Expenses for temporarily leasing equipment, machinery and infrastructure, providing operational flexibility without long-term financial commitments (below 12 months).. Building renovation measures: This category includes non-capitalized costs related to building renovation measures carried out to ensure the continued and effective functioning of the asset.. Day-to-day servicing of assets of property, plant and equipment (PPE): Expenditures related to the ongoing operation, upkeep and management of the Group's physical assets to ensure their safe and efficient use.

###### Numerator

The numerator consists of the portion of OpEx included in the denominator that meets one of the following conditions:. Associated with Taxonomy-aligned assets or processes, including research and development for cleaner energy solutions, workforce training on sustainability practices and human resource adaptation.. Linked to the purchase of output from Taxonomy-aligned economic activities or individual measures enabling target activities to reduce greenhouse gas emissions or transition to low-carbon operations.

If OpEx is not material to the business model of a non-financial undertaking, the following disclosures apply:. The numerator of the OpEx KPI is set to zero, as per Annex I, section 1.1.3.2 of Delegated Regulation (EU) 2021/2178.. The total OpEx denominator value is disclosed.. A qualitative explanation is provided to justify the non-materiality of operational expenditures in the business model.
