## 3 CLIMATE CHANGE [E]]

### 3.1 Strategy

#### 3.1.1 Transition plan for Climate Change Mitigation [E1-1]

MOTOR OIL Group is dedicated to promoting sustainable development in alignment with key global initiatives such as the European Green Deal, the Paris Agreement and the United Nations' Sustainable Development Goals.

Decarbonization and Energy Transition is the foundation of the MOTOR OIL Group's strategic plan with the aim of becoming an Integrated Energy company trusted by its stakeholders.

In response to challenges, a strategic priority is to reduce carbon emissions in Group's activities and to increase the portfolio of climate resilience for the next decade, looking forward to net-zero in 2050. Driven by the latest global developments, the Group's decarbonization strategy aims to mitigate the effects and adapt to climate change, while committing to energy transition and low-carbon economy by setting ambitious carbon reduction targets.

MOTOR OIL Group is committed to deliver an ambitious Energy Transition plan which will transform the Group into a Sustainable, Diverse, Multi-Energy leader in Southeastern Europe. This involves investments in new sustainable technologies, leading the way in renewable energy penetration to Group portfolio and enhancing energy efficiency for Group's own activities and customers.

MOTOR OIL Group's goal is to create services and solutions for its customers and long-term value for its shareholders by investing in the energy transition, preparing for a shifting Energy market.

##### Climate goals and alignment with global targets

Group's commitment to sustainability is resolute and is reflected as mentioned above in the strategic plan. In this context, MOTOR OIL Group has set a net zero goal by 2050. Using 2021 as the baseline year, MOTOR OIL Group has set climate reduction targets by 2030, as outlined below:





<table>
<tr>
<th></th>
<th rowspan="2">LL Target</th>
<th></th>
</tr>
<tr>
<th>Goal</th>
<th>Timeframe</th>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>30% Reduction</td>
<td>By 2030</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Scope 3 emissions (2021 baseline/ according to Net-zero scenario)</td>

</tr>
</table>

To achieve these ambitious targets, MOTOR OIL Group has developed a detailed roadmap outlining the necessary measures to deliver the required absolute savings in its industrial activities. The Group continuously benchmarks its approach against the targets and plans announced by industry peers, ensuring alignment with best practices and industry standards. Furthermore, the Group acknowledges its role in contributing to a sustainable energy mix. As part of this commitment, MOTOR OIL Group prioritizes producing power with low carbon intensity, thereby further reducing its environmental footprint and promoting a cleaner energy transition.

In the short-term (1 to 5 years), MOTOR OIL Group is navigating a period of rapid transformation along with an evolving geopolitical context marked by increasing policy pressures, energy security concerns and shifting alliances. The immediate focus is on substantial investments in low-carbon and renewable energy projects such as solar, wind, hydrogen, as these are becoming integral to its core strategy. This rapid shift is driven by both the urgency of climate commitments and the geopolitical landscape where energy independence and security are becoming priorities for many countries. As traditional oil and gas markets face volatility due to geopolitical tensions, sanctions and trade policies, the Group is forced to accelerate divestment from high-carbon assets and reinvest in cleaner, locally viable energy solutions. Financially, the Group adopts active strategies to reallocate resources efficiently, managing both the risks and opportunities arising from this accelerated shift, mitigate risks associated with geopolitical instability while capturing opportunities in new, more environmentally friendly markets, aligning closely with evolving regulatory frameworks and carbon pricing mechanisms. The short-term strategic financial planning is explicitly outlined in the MOTOR OIL's "Strategy & Outlook Targets 2030".

The medium-term horizon (5 to 15 years) builds on this transformative momentum by scaling up and integrating these new environmentally friendly initiatives into the MOTOR OIL Group's operations and identity. This phase focuses on deepening the transition as the Group starts to mature as a diversified energy player. It involves ramping up large-scale renewable energy projects and clean technology solutions, expanding into areas such as green hydrogen and carbon capture and storage. In this term MOTOR OIL Group needs to be adaptable to changing geopolitical realities, such as regional energy transitions, shifts in global trade routes and new bilateral or multilateral agreements. In addition, the medium term is characterized by driving operational and technological innovation to reduce costs, enhance efficiencies and achieve greater scalability in clean energy projects. Financial planning is centered around ensuring sustainable growth, with a focus on leveraging new revenue streams from low-carbon businesses and reinforcing MOTOR OIL Group's position as a competitive and resilient entity in a future energy landscape.

The long-term horizon (15 to 25 years) sees MOTOR OIL Group reaching the peak of its transformation journey, aiming for net-zero emissions and establishing itself as a leading force in sustainable energy. The strategy shifts from growth to consolidation, with a focus on complete decarbonization across the value chain. By this stage, MOTOR OIL Group is not just an energy provider but a key player in national sustainability efforts, leveraging its diversified portfolio to offer integrated solutions that combine renewable energy, alternative fuels and circular economy principle. Financial strategies in the long \- term horizon rely on innovative instruments such as green bonds, sustainability-linked financing and long-term impact investments to support continued growth in a low-carbon economy. The Group's role involves shaping the future energy landscape, influencing international negotiations and fostering cross-border collaborations that align with both climate goals and geopolitical realities.



<!-- Report Page 152 -->
##### Key steps and actions per Scope 1,2,3 and as per company

MOTOR OIL Group demonstrates tangibly its commitment to reduce its carbon footprint and thus contribute to the sustainable protection and preservation of the environment. Through its strategic plan, it is focused on decarbonization of its own activities and its product portfolio, by undertaking and participating in the design and implementation of innovative research projects. The Group is actively working to minimize its carbon emissions across all facets of its operations through strategic investments, innovative technologies, and forward-thinking initiatives. The Group is committed to expanding its renewables business, which will lower the carbon intensity of its energy generation. Through these concerted efforts, MOTOR OIL Group remains dedicated to achieving its emissions reduction targets and advancing its sustainability goals in line with global climate objectives. The transition plan includes:. Carbon capture and storage project. Green Hydrogen production

· Sustainable production of E-fuels. Advanced biofuels and recycled fuel production. Renewable energy production with solar and wind power. Acquisitions and investments in new, low carbon projects. Transition energy through CCGT plant and FSRU. E-mobility development with an increased number of EV charging stations. Energy efficiency optimization. On site RES and Battery storage installations. Innovative processing and digital technologies

#### Emblematic projects

MOTOR OIL Group is driven by its determination to contribute to the preservation of the environment, energy and digital transition through the participation in EU and national co-funded programs such as:

BLUE MED is an integrated large-scale investment project that demonstrates an industrial ecosystem of ultra-low carbon hydrogen production and supply as a clean energy vector. This ecosystem is integrated with a scalable and flexible supply chain network that will deliver the hydrogen safely and reliably to end consumers throughout Greece and internationally. In the context of Blue Med, the following two sub-projects have been submitted for funding in EU and national funding programs and schemes:

IRIS (CCUS) project, funded by the EU Innovation Fund, is a flagship initiative of MOTOR OIL for the decarbonization of the Agioi Theodoroi Refinery in Corinthia, Greece, with significant impact on a national, regional and European scale. IRIS project is an important step towards achieving the industrial goals for environmental protection and enhances the effort for sustainable energy production, which makes it a pioneer at national and European level. This is a low carbon hydrogen and methanol production by large scale carbon capture project which comprises an innovative, heavily integrated point-source CCUS technology solution, applied on its current hydrogen production process and its coupling to a small-scale methanol production unit, which will utilize part of the captured CO2 as feedstock.

The project has been granted in 2023 for 127 m€ from Innovation Fund. The project 's carbon capture solution on Refinery's Steam Methane Reforming Unit (SMR) reducing its GHG emissions by capturing 495.6 kMT CO2 demonstrating an industrial ecosystem of ultra-low carbon hydrogen (blue H2) production and supply it as a clean energy vector.

The Hydrogen Stream's GHG emissions are expected to be reduced by 89% (net) effectively transforming its currently produced grey hydrogen to blue with a footprint of approximately 1,894 tCO2e/H2 over the whole value chain (from capture to storage), well below the taxonomy compliance limit of 3 t CO2/Mt H2 and an estimated Blue H2 production of 55,280 t/year. At the same time, part of the captured CO2 will be combined with electrolytic H2 produced by an electrolyzer (see EPHYRA project) on premises to produce e-methanol as a low-carbon energy carrier for mobility purposes and other industrial usage. The methanol production will be demonstrated at a small scale (10,000 t/year) to assess its integration in the plant and pave the way for further scaling-up in order, to a) increase the utilization of the amount of captured CO2, b) develop e-methanol into a major output product (e-fuel). The remaining captured CO2 will be compressed, liquefied and transported by ship for final storage. In view of the increased energy needs, the project will be supplied for the 17,6 MW of extra power required from its wind and solar parks developed by its subsidiary Motor Oil Renewable Energy (MORE). Further to that, MOTOR OIL will seek to achieve the highest level of energy integration with the refinery to minimize the energy needs of the project.

It has to be noted that the planned industrial growth initiatives will lead to an incremental increase of approximately 200 kilotons in Scope 1 & 2 emissions by 2030. To balance this increase and achieve an overall emissions reduction target, the Group has outlined a comprehensive strategy, leveraging process and efficiency optimization. These measures, estimated to contribute around 350 kMT CO2. include fuel switch and energy efficiency measures concerning the refinery operations. Fuel switch measures concern hydrogen and biomethane use for heat and power generation and electrification of equipment (heaters and boilers). The project receives funding from the Innovation Fund program under Grant Agreement No. 101133015. (https://www.iris-ccus-project.eu)

GREEN HYDROGEN Project involves the design, construction and installation of infrastructure to produce renewable hydrogen via electrolysis at MOTOR OIL's industrial facility in Agioi Theodoroi, Corinth and its compression and transportation/distribution to end-users. This will be the first large-scale renewable hydrogen production facility in Greece. More specifically, the Green Hydrogen Project, which has already received funding by EU mechanisms and programs (RRF, Horizon Europe/Clean Hydrogen Partnership, Connecting Europe Facility - Transport, EIB Loan) has the following scope: design and engineering of an 30MW (up-scalable to 50MW) electrolyzer and ancillary/related infrastructure; establishment of a novel logistics system for the compression and distribution of renewable hydrogen within Greece; investment in a new hydrogen network in the industrial facility leading to a new compression terminal, conditioning and metering.

The following EU funded projects co-support the development of the Green Hydrogen Project:

I. TRIERES project aims at creating a small-scale Hydrogen Valley with geographical reference to the MOTOR OIL Refinery of Ag. Theodoroi in Corinth. That is a hydrogen ecosystem in Greece, linking the green hydrogen production unit that the company is developing there within the EPHYRA project with businesses, technology providers, academic and research institutions, public sector organisations and local government bodies, becoming potential off takers. The project provides an important opportunity for the implementation of hydrogen pilot projects (city buses, passenger vehicles, shipping, power plant) and studies of hydrogen use for existing or future applications and synergies with existing and future hydrogen valleys in the EU (Netherlands, Austria, Cyprus) and Egypt. The TRIERES Valley has a tremendous upward perspective over a large part of the Balkans, SouthEastern Europe and the wider area of Eastern Mediterranean. The project started in July 2023, is implemented by a consortium of 26 partners from 5 different countries and is expected to be completed by the end of April 2028. This project is supported by the Clean Hydrogen Partnership and its members Hydrogen Europe and Hydrogen Europe Research under Grant Agreement No.
10111056. (https://www.trieres-h2.eu)



II. EPHYRA is a highly innovative and pioneering project as it will demonstrate an innovative hydrogen production facility at industrial scale, from renewable energy sources, by employing improved electrolysis technology. The large-scale electrolysis will be integrated with industrial operations within Motor Oil's Corinth Refinery, one of the top refineries in Europe and the largest privately-owned industrial complex in Greece. The project started in June 2023, is implemented by a consortium of 10 partners from 7 different countries and is expected to be completed by the end of May 2028. The project is supported by the Clean Hydrogen Partnership and its members Hydrogen Europe and Hydrogen Europe Research under Grant Agreement No. 101112220. (https://ephyraproject.eu/)

III. REA for the construction of the 1st publicly accessible HRS (Hydrogen Refueling Station) in Agioi Theodoroi (Corinth, Peloponnese, Greece). The project started in November 2021, is implemented by AVINOIL and is expected to be completed by April 2025. The project receives funding from the Connecting Europe Facility - Transport program under Grant Agreement No. 101079451.

IV. REAH2 for the construction of the 2nd publicly accessible HRS (Hydrogen Refueling Station) in Akrata (Peloponnese, Greece). The project started in April 2023, is implemented by AVINOIL and is expected to be completed in Q4 2025. The project receives funding from the Connecting Europe Facility - Transport program under Grant Agreement No. 101119200.

V. REAH3 for the construction of the 3rd HRS for public transport means - buses in the bus depot of OSY at Thriasio, Attiki. The project started in November 2023, is implemented by AVINOIL and is expected to be completed in Q4 2026. The project receives funding from the Connecting Europe Facility - Transport program under Grant Agreement No. 101165972.

MOTOR OIL Group's EU-funded decarbonization projects are built on the belief that real change happens when diverse stakeholders come together - each bringing their own perspective, expertise and drive for a more sustainable future. DIORIGA GAS FSRU is a new Liquified Natural Gas (LNG) import terminal based on the Floating Storage and Regasification Unit (FSRU) configuration being developed by Dioriga Gas S.A., a fully owned subsidiary of MOTOR OIL Group. It is expected to strengthen the Greek NG network, eliminate EU dependence on Russian fossil fuels (REPowerEU objective) and ultimately enhance energy diversification, even for the non-connected to the NG network areas, via the use of LNG as a cleaner, affordable and secure transition fuel. The infrastructure supporting the FSRU shall incorporate the ability to accommodate renewable fuels, including hydrogen. The FSRU and all related installations will be supplied with electricity from land (cold ironing) from renewable energy sources, ensuring zero carbon operations. The project has received the status of "Strategic Investment" under respective national law.

#### Investments in emission reductions

The Group aims to invest more than 4 billion euros by 2030 in executing one of the largest energy transition programs in the region. These investments will be allocated across various initiatives aimed at driving sustainability, reducing carbon emissions (RES development, petrochemicals, biofuels, natural gas, hydrogen, decarbonization) and enhancing resilience (facilities maintenance, logistical improvements, digitalisation and efficiency & HSSE projects).

These investments represent a commitment to delivering tangible results in the Group's transition towards a more sustainable energy future by 2030.

Moreover, in 2024 capital expenditure (Capex), investments in environmentally sustainable activities aligned with taxonomy standards resulted to €139,802,346.08 accounting for 44.38% of the total Capex of the Group. Additionally, operational expenditure (Opex) on environmentally sustainable activities aligned with taxonomy standards amounted to €30,155,553.22, constituting 20.92% of the total Opex incurred by the group. Finally, as part of Group's resilience program aimed at strengthening energy security and infrastructure development, investments in oil and gas-related economic activities during the reporting period amounted to approximately €200 million.

#### Assessment of locked-in emissions

Carbon Lock-in is a path-dependence phenomenon consisting of an inertial resistance to the transition process to carbon-efficient technologies as entire sectors continue relying on carbon-intensive technologies. Carbon Lock-in is the outcome of highly path-dependent decision processes related to carbon-emitting technologies, infrastructure, practices and their supporting networks that are currently constraining future paths, making it more challenging, even impossible, to subsequently pursue more optimal paths toward low-carbon objectives. In path-dependent processes, inertial resistance to large-scale systematic shifts is self-reinforced with time, due to initial favorable social and economic conditions driving increasing returns, thereby diminishing and possibly excluding more efficient alternatives, as any locked-in system aims at maintaining the status quo. As a matter of fact, Carbon Lock-in generates constant market and policy failures that discourage the implementation of carbon-saving technologies regardless of their high-cost efficiency or environmental advantages: the result is that the decarbonization process is ineffective due to the high difficulty in eroding the deep dependencies that perpetuate the lock-in state.

Concretely, the International Energy Agency (IEA) and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) discovered that constant investment in carbon-emitting technologies instead of more carbonefficient alternatives would cause the investment cost to increase fourfold in the longer term, while reducing the chance to achieve ambitious climate goals. In this sense, investing in assets prone to lockin increases the costs of achieving agreed climate protection goals considering that carbon-intensive investments could not be usually easily converted into a more sustainable solution. This generates a high Carbon Lock-in risk, as it is reinforced once a carbon-intensive technology keeps operating even if economically preferable lower-carbon alternatives exist for its replacement.

MOTOR OIL Group is committed to developing a Carbon Lock-in Risk methodology, engaging in a comprehensive analysis of the existing frameworks. The methodology's scope encompasses the complete range of business activities, including subsidiaries; for the refinery, representing more than the 97% of overall emissions and considering the specific characteristics of the sector and the lack of a sectoral benchmark, a specific approach has been developed, in coherence with IEA and NZE Scenario.

The methodology is developed not only for determining Group's existing carbon locked-in emissions in business activities, but for being a decision-making tool for future investments aligned to the climate scenarios and to guide its transition plan: the level of Carbon Lock-in risk will be used to pivot attention and corrective measures on those business activities that are rated as at high risk of Carbon Lock-in.

The Carbon Lock-in methodology includes critical assumptions made to determine the dynamic mechanism of its evaluation along with the critical assumptions made to determine these risks. This includes the source of the applied third-party evaluation and the rationale behind their relevance for the chosen application.

MOTOR OIL will ensure to enhance the quality of the assessment, increasing the quality of data and their granularity for the next years. MOTOR OIL is engaging in updating the methodology every year reflecting, among other things, lessons learned by its use, the experience of other stakeholders, regulatory requirements and the development of external tools and reference works.

The Assessment of the Carbon Lock-In Risk is aligned with the scope of the Corporate Sustainability Reporting and includes all business activities of MOTOR OIL and its subsidiaries. For each company included in the assessment the relevant business activities have been identified, including all activities listed as "EU Taxonomy eligible" and defining categories for "EU Taxonomy non eligible". It is based on a stepwise approach; assessment of each step is carried out sequentially and it is dependent on the outcomes of the previous one.

The interdependency between the steps is a crucial characteristic of the methodology, as specific results of the assessment can lead to an overall evaluation of the business activity as "low risk of Carbon Lock-in" (i.e. if the technical assessment steps results in a low technical Carbon Lock-in risk related, there is no necessity to move forward to the institutional assessment step; similarly, a low institutional Carbon Lock-in risk hinders the implementation of the economic assessment).

The methodology considers three types of risks:

a) technical/sectoral; b) institutional; c) financial.

MOTOR OIL's strategy is coherent with sectoral decarbonization as the Group strategy considers several actions to reduce CO2 emissions and these are related to low carbon products, biofuels and RES and the Group is transitioning to a new model. Furthermore, the EU policies in force (EU-ETS) support and incentivize the efforts of the Group.

### Internal Carbon Pricing assessment

MOTOR OIL Group is committed to developing an Internal Carbon Pricing (ICP) methodology to mitigate risk, to hedge against future energy price increases and to better align with business opportunities in the transition to a low-carbon economy aligning to the Paris Agreement goals. Also, an Internal Carbon Pricing Program (ICPP) will be implemented to internalize the external cost of climate change in the broadest possible range of economic decision making and in setting economic incentives for clean and sustainable development. The ICP will be integrated with the related Assessment of carbon lockedin emissions and the Climate Scenarios by the end of 2025.

The ICP includes critical assumptions made to determine the dynamic mechanism of price definition along with the critical assumptions will be made to determine these prices; ICPs are based on scientific guidance and how the methodology foresees to estimate their future values along with science-based carbon pricing trajectories. In designing the Program, the Group also considers the management system to monitor and evaluate how ICPs are consistent with financial statements, especially concerning asset valuation and impairment assessments. The ICPP will enable MOTOR OIL to make decarbonization, a company-wide effort through internal carbon fees and incorporating carbon pricing data as an input to other financial evaluative measures and to the assessment of the Carbon Lock-in Risk. Carbon price applied according to the type of scheme and any relevant critical assumptions made to calculate it, will be available after the planned assessment that is to be conducted.

The methodology covers Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions of all business activities (eligible and not eligible for the EU Taxonomy) and sets exclusion for the calculation of ICP based on threshold for some non-relevant activities in terms of emission. Activities covered with a carbon price, namely under the EU-ETS or the ETS2 in the specific case, are included in the methodology with a specific approach as the ICP is represented by the market value of the allowances. For all business activities, the methodology foresees an assessment of the existence an explicit, implicit (including shadow) carbon price, with the scope of determining the Marginal Costs of Abatement (MACs) and the comparison with alternative scenarios, including the ones presented in the official Strategy document of MOTOR OIL and the ones aligned to the NZE scenario. Subsequently, the methodology identifies how to prioritize ICP, based on the significance of the MOTOR OIL Group's parameters, namely the overall strategy, its material topics and the financial cash-flow. Finally, for the definition of the ICPP the methodology includes a clear process to integrate the ICPs as a shadow cost of carbon for investment evaluation and as a tool to guide the decarbonization strategy.

### Compliance with climate regulations

#### EU Taxonomy

To ensure full compliance with the Taxonomy Regulation, MOTOR OIL Group conducted an evaluation of both eligibility and alignment by thoroughly assessing its economic activities against the six environmental objectives outlined in the Climate Delegated Act and the Environmental Delegated Act. Based on these assessments, the Group calculated the turnover, capital expenditure (CapEx) and operational expenditure (OpEx) associated with each identified eligible and aligned economic activity. The scope of this analysis encompasses all consolidated subsidiaries of MOTOR OIL Group, as reported in the financial statement, in line with Regulation 2020/852 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 18 June 2020.

#### EU ETS

Oil Refining is included in the EU-ETS 1; for the sector a specific methodology for the attribution of free allowances was established. In the CWT (CO2 weighted tonne) approach a factor is assigned to each refinery process unit. Each unit's CWT factor is the ratio of the unit's CO2 emissions to the CO2 emissions of a crude distillation unit, with both units operating under "standard" conditions, i.e. at a standard level of energy performance and using a standard fuel. The refinery-wide CWT is the sum, over all the refinery units, of the product of each unit's CWT factor by the corresponding unit throughput over a given time period. An additional term accounts for other refinery activities outside of process units, such as blending, storage etc. A refinery's CWT is an activity function that correlates well with refinery's CO2 emissions at a standard level of CO2 emissions performance, while its real CO2 emissions reflect the actual level of CO2 performance of the refinery. To ensure consistency between emission sources included in actual emissions and in CWT and to comply with the specific requirements of the EU ETS Directive regarding electricity (no free allowance to be granted to electricity production) corrections had to be applied to both emissions (starting from the verified site emissions reported under the EU ETS) and CWT. Oil refining benchmark is defined by the Regulation (0,228 tonCO2/CWT, to be updated in Q1 2026) and set the level of free emissions of the refinery. Nevertheless, for the evaluation of the transition plan and the alignment to the Paris Agreement goals, the IEA Net Zero approach can be used.

Concerning Carbon Lock-in Risk (CLR): Oil refining accounts for more than 97% of the Group's total emissions and, hypothetically, could represent the highest-risk asset in terms of carbon lock-in. However, based on the Carbon Lock-in Risk assessment, this activity is classified as low risk, since its emissions are regulated under the EU Emissions Trading System (EU ETS). Furthermore, the inclusion of oil refining in the EU ETS, along with the compliance obligations imposed by the relevant regulation, ensures a low institutional risk of carbon lock-in. As such, the institutional assessment phase assigns a LOW rating, and the overall risk outcome remains LOW.

Concerning Internal Carbon Pricing (ICP): Within the ICP the Marginal Abatement Cost (MAC) will be represented by the market price that will be compared to possible solutions to reduce CO2 emissions with a lower cost.

The sale of petroleum products is (partially) covered by the new ETS2. As part of the 2023 revisions of the ETS Directive, a new emissions trading system named ETS2 was created, separate from the existing EU ETS. This new system will cover and address the CO2 emissions from fuel combustion in buildings, road transport and additional sectors (mainly small industry not covered by the existing EU ETS). The ETS2 will complement other policies of the European Green Deal in the covered sectors, helping Member States achieve their emission reduction targets under the Effort Sharing Regulation (ESR). So far, emission reductions in those sectors have been insufficient to put the EU on a firm path towards its 2050 climate neutrality goal. The carbon price set by the ETS2 will provide a market incentive for investments in building renovations and low-emissions mobility. The ETS2 will become fully operational in 2027. Although it will be a 'cap and trade' system like the existing EU ETS, the ETS2 will cover emissions upstream. All emission allowances in the ETS2 will be auctioned, without any free allocation.

During the first three years the ETS2 is operational, if the price of allowances exceeds €45 (in 2020 prices, i.e. adjusted for inflation), additional allowances may be released from the ETS2 market stability reserve to address excessive price increases. Allowances may also be released from this reserve if the price of allowances increases too rapidly. The rules and conditions for such a release are specified in the ETS Directive. In case of exceptionally high gas or oil prices in 2026, the start of the ETS2 system could be postponed to 2028 to ensure a smooth implementation.

The MOTOR OIL entities regulated under the ETS2 will be required to surrender sufficient allowances to cover their emissions. As in the ETS2 the allowances will be sold and purchase in auctions and no free allowances will be given to regulated entity, it is necessary to forecast the budget of emissions covered by the ETS2 and allocate the proceed to be compliant with the Regulation.

Concerning CLR: the institutional assessment phase will set the risk as LOW and the final result will be always LOW.

Concerning ICP: in the ICP the MAC will be represented by the market price that will be compared to possible solutions to reduce CO2 emissions with a lower cost.

##### National Climate Law

The National Climate Law (4936/2022 (A' 105)) - "Transition to climate neutrality and adaptation to climate change, emergency provisions for addressing the energy crisis and protecting the environment" was issued in 2022. The aim is to create a framework for improving the country's adaptive capacity, climate resilience and the gradual transition to climate neutrality by 2050. In order to achieve the longterm goal of climate neutrality, intermediate climate targets are set for the years 2030 and 2040 as follows: Reduction of net anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions by at least 55% in 2030 and by 80% in 2040 compared to 1990 levels. The National Energy and Climate Plan (NECP) is taken into account in achieving the targets.

Articles 19 and 20 define the obligated parties (companies and installations). Companies subject to article 20 are obliged to report annually their carbon footprint emissions. Motor Oil, NRG, MORE and Coral Gas are the MOTOR OIL's Group companies subject to article 20. In 2024 the second carbon footprint report was submitted from the referred companies. The report includes scope 1 & 2 and is verified.

Respectively, installations covered from article 19 except those that are under ETS schemes, are required to reduce emissions by at least thirty percent (30%) by 2030 compared to 2019, by reference to the appropriate product and project unit, depending on the type of activity. According to competent authorities' instructions, by the end of 2025 for all existing projects and activities, a report shall be submitted to the competent environmental licensing authority to demonstrate compliance with the emission reduction target. From 2026, the operator of the project or activity shall submit to the licensing environmental authority by 31 October of each year.

#### Fossil fuel activities

MOTOR OIL Group's goal is to create services and solutions for customers and long-term value for its shareholders by investing diligently in the energy transition, preparing for a shifting Energy market. The Group is enhancing the efficiency and sustainability of the refinery operations, leveraging digitization and innovation to minimize its carbon footprint, while ensuring the energy security of the country in a rapidly evolving geopolitical landscape.

MOTOR OIL Group's refinery supports the energy transition reliably with products that the world needs now and until new sustainable technologies mature. To ensure improved and reliable operation of the refinery, the Group is continuously investing in initiatives that can optimize the refinery's performance, upgrade its products and reduce its environmental footprint. Key actions for this improvement are:

1\. 160,000 tpa propylene production unit diversifying the refinery's product mix. Construction to be completed by 2025

2\. Storage capacity expansion to be completed by 2025 (+40,000 m3)

3\. New port facilities (Athina) to materially expand processing, storage and trading capacity (+5 mn tons throughput potential)

4\. Development of 250,000+ tpa advanced biofuel production program

#### Integration with corporate strategy

MOTOR OIL climate change transition plan is a strategic roadmap fully integrated into the Group's overarching business strategy and long-term financial planning. It reflects the Group's commitment to align with the European Green Deal, Fit for 55 targets and evolving regulatory frameworks, including the EU Taxonomy and CSRD. The plan supports MOTOR OIL's transformation from a conventional refining company into a diversified, low-carbon energy group, while maintaining business resilience and profitability.

The transition plan sets clear decarbonization milestones, with more than €2.5 billion, as part of the €4 billion total, in planned investments by 2030 to enable structural changes across operations, product offerings and energy systems. A key component is the expansion of renewable electricity generation to 2 GW by 2030, supporting the Group's goal of producing clean power and achieving energy independence.

The plan also prioritizes the production of sustainable fuels, including the commissioning of a 250 ktpa advanced biofuel production program, 60 ktpa of low-carbon hydrogen and 25 ktpa of e-methanol by 2029. These fuels are expected to support the decarbonization of transport, shipping and industry. Additionally, the Group aims to expand its biodiesel capacity to 200 ktpa through co-processing of biogenic feedstocks, advancing circular economy objectives.

Importantly, the transition plan incorporates a Carbon Capture and Storage (CCS) project designed to capture and permanently store CO2 emissions from refinery operations. This initiative will be a critical enabler for reducing Scope 1 emissions and achieving long-term carbon neutrality targets. The CCS project is aligned with the EU Innovation Fund priorities and demonstrates the Group's proactive approach to hard-to-abate sector decarbonization.

The transition plan is embedded in financial planning, with expected shifts in capital allocation, new revenue streams and changes in EBITDA composition. By 2030, over 40% of Group EBITDA is projected to come from non-fossil activities. Investments will be financed through a mix of retained earnings, projectlevel financing and sustainability-linked financial instruments, ensuring long-term viability and value creation for investors, customers and other stakeholders.

### Leadership approval and governance

The Board of Directors and management have an important role in supporting the long-term success of the business while considering climate-related risks that might impact operations and value for shareholders.

The Group has formed a Sustainability Committee with the goal of approving and overseeing the Group's Sustainable Development Policy and Strategy, as well as monitoring performance against set targets and deciding on corrective actions in relation to the opportunities or risks that may exist.

### Progress reporting and transparency

Climate-related metrics play a pivotal role within the MOTOR OIL Group's governance, strategy and risk management framework, forming an integral part of its decision-making processes.

These metrics are treated with the same level of importance as other key performance indicators (KPIs) and key risk indicators (KRIs), creating a feedback loop that evolves over time. By incorporating climate related metrics into its governance structure, the Group gains valuable insights into the potential impacts of climate-related risks and opportunities across various aspects of its operations. These metrics help the Group anticipate and address financial and operational implications associated with climate risks and opportunities over specific time frames. Moreover, whenever possible and relevant, the Group includes indicators related to GHG Emissions, Energy Efficiency, Carbon intensity, Climate Risk Exposure, sustainable investments according to EU taxonomy (climate mitigation and adaptation).

MOTOR OIL Group has conducted an internal assessment in accordance with ESRS E1.16(g) to determine whether the undertaking meets the eligibility criteria for inclusion in the EU Paris-Aligned Benchmarks (PAB), as defined in Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2022/2453 and the Climate Benchmark Regulation (EU 2020/1818).

Based on this assessment, MOTOR OIL Group has evaluated its revenue composition, business activities and emissions profile in relation to the exclusion thresholds outlined in Article 12.1 and Article 12.2 of the Climate Benchmark Regulation.

According to Article 12.1, companies are excluded from the PAB if they meet any of the following conditions:. Deriving 1% or more of revenues from coal and lignite extraction, mining, or distribution.. Deriving 10% or more of revenues from oil exploration, extraction, distribution, or refining.. Deriving 50% or more of revenues from natural gas exploration, extraction, manufacturing, or distribution.. Deriving 50% or more of revenues from electricity generation with a GHG intensity greater than 100 gCO2e/kWh.

The Group operates in the oil refining, fuel trading and energy production sectors, with significant revenue generated from oil refining, fossil fuel distribution and natural gas trading. Given these activities, MOTOR OIL Group currently exceeds the exclusion thresholds for oil and gas-related revenues, which impacts its eligibility for inclusion in the Paris-Aligned Benchmark.

Additionally, Article 12.2 states that benchmark administrators must exclude companies that are estimated to significantly harm environmental objectives, as defined by the EU Taxonomy Regulation (EU 2020/852). MOTOR OIL Group has been assessing the alignment of its operations with the EU ParisAligned Benchmarks (PAB) since 2022. This evaluation follows the criteria set by Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2022/2453 and the Climate Benchmark Regulation (EU 2020/1818).

Although the Group is currently excluded from the PAB, it recognizes the importance of aligning with global climate investment benchmarks and remains committed to achieving long-term sustainability and financial resilience.

## Transition KPIs /Milestones

MOTOR OIL Group has developed a robust transition plan underpinned by quantifiable KPIs and clear forward-looking milestones, reflecting its commitment to decarbonisation, renewable energy expansion and the development of sustainable fuels.

Key Performance Indicators for the reporting year (KPIs). CO2e emissions avoided through renewable energy sources: 820,668 tonnes

### Milestones

MOTOR OIL has set clear transition milestones and KPIs as part of its decarbonisation and circular economy strategy. The Group aims to develop 2 GW of renewable energy capacity by 2030, reinforcing its position in clean electricity generation. It plans to commission an advanced biofuel production facility with over 250 ktpa capacity, supporting low-carbon transport alternatives. In the area of hydrogen, the Group targets approximately 60 ktpa of low-carbon hydrogen production by 2029, supported by CCS technology and aligned with industrial decarbonisation needs. Similarly, around 25 ktpa of e-methanol production is expected by 2029, reflecting growing market demand in the shipping and chemical sectors. Moreover, biodiesel production capacity is set to reach 200 ktpa through the co-processing of biogenic feedstocks, reinforcing the Group's commitment to circular economy principles and sustainable fuels.

#### 3.1.2 Material Impacts, Risks and Opportunities and their interaction with Strategy and Business Model [E1.SBM-3]

MOTOR OIL Group has undertaken a comprehensive assessment of material climate-related impacts, risks and opportunities across its value chain. The Group recognizes that its operations, particularly oil refining and fuel distribution, contribute significantly to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, energy consumption, air and water pollution, biodiversity degradation and hazardous waste generation. These are acknowledged as long-term negative environmental impacts arising from core industrial activities. These impacts are long-term in nature and material across multiple business areas.

At the same time, positive climate-related impacts are being realized through growing investments in renewable energy projects (such as solar and wind farms), biofuels, green hydrogen and energy efficiency technologies. These investments help reduce emissions, improve environmental outcomes and support the Group's shift toward sustainable energy solutions.

The Group has identified a range of climate-related risks and opportunities affecting its key business sectors, including Refining, Biofuels, Renewable Energy Sources (RES) Power, Hydrogen Production and Natural Gas Trade etc. These are evaluated across short- (1-5 years), medium- (5-15 years) and longterm (15-25 years) horizons. This time horizon refers to the transition plan and differs from time horizon as describes in the ESRS framework (Additional Information [BP-2]). Please also note that these climaterelated risks have been identified in the context of Climate - related Scenario Analysis that the Group has conducted.

Transition Risks stem from the evolving policy and regulatory landscape (e.g., the EU Green Deal, Fit for 55), technological disruption, shifting market demands and reputational pressures linked to carbonintensive operations.

Physical Risks include both acute threats such as flooding, storms and heatwaves and chronic pressures such as sea-level rise and temperature variability, which pose challenges to infrastructure, supply chains and operational continuity.

Opportunities arise from expanding into clean energy markets - renewables, green hydrogen, biofuels and energy trading - and from embracing circular economy models and sustainable construction practices.

These identified impacts and risks are not treated in isolation but form a critical input into the Group's forward-looking strategy. MOTOR OIL is actively transforming from a traditional oil and gas operator into a multi-energy provider committed to decarbonization, innovation and circular economy. Its ambition is to achieve net-zero emissions by 2050.

This transformation is guided by scenario-based resilience assessments, ensuring the Group remains agile and responsive under various climate and market futures. This forward-thinking approach positions MOTOR OIL Group to not only mitigate its environmental footprint but also capitalize on emerging opportunities in a transitioning global energy landscape.

##### Climate resilience analysis

###### Scope

MOTOR OIL Group fully acknowledges the significant environmental impact its operations - and those of its subsidiaries - can have. While various aspects of the Group's activities contribute to atmospheric emissions, the organization is firmly committed to addressing these impacts with transparency, responsibility and a forward-looking approach.

Inresponse to the growing complexity of climate-related challenges, the Group conducts comprehensive resilience analyses to evaluate its exposure to both physical and transition risks associated with climate change. This analysis encompasses the Group's core business operations and extends across the value chain - covering both upstream and downstream activities.

The Group's climate risk screening process spans nearly 100% of its operational sites, ensuring that all location-specific climate vulnerabilities - including those related to assets and business functions - are rigorously assessed. This evaluation is supported by a combination of internal data and leading external sources, including:. The IPCC 5th and 6th Assessment Reports. Regional Climate Change Adaptation Plans. Socio-economic scenarios from the Network for Greening the Financial System (NGFS). The Greek National Energy and Climate Plan (NECP). The S&P Global Climanomics Platform, which supports the Group's proprietary Climate Risk Assessment

By integrating these scientific and policy-driven tools, MOTOR OIL Group is well-positioned to proactively identify, assess and manage climate-related risks across its portfolio. This robust approach reinforces the Group's resilience and strengthens its positioning as a leader in the energy transition. As such, no material physical or transition risks were excluded from the analysis.

<!-- Report Page 166 -->
###### Scenario Analysis

The Group conducts scenario analysis to assess the potential impacts of climate change on its operations, supply chain and market dynamics. These analyses help the Group enhance its adaptation and mitigation strategies, ensuring alignment with global climate objectives and regulatory frameworks. By evaluating different climate scenarios, including those consistent with a 1.5℃ global warming trajectory, the Group can proactively identify financial, operational and strategic risks and opportunities associated with the transition to a low-carbon economy. Further details on the scenario analysis methodology and key findings are provided in Chapter E1, IRO-1 of this report, where a comprehensive assessment of climate resilience is outlined.

MOTOR OIL Group conducts an analysis of GHG emissions pathways and temperature scenarios to assess potential future impacts on its operations and the broader business environment.

The methodology of MOTOR OIL Group's scenarios analysis consists of two basic steps.

1\. The first step includes the definition of the time horizons and the scenarios used as well as the drivers in each scenario. The drivers include climate, commercial and geopolitical aspects, regulatory compliance and carbon pricing. Furthermore, an uncertainty impact assessment of each driver is included.

2\. The second step is evaluating the resilience of Group's portfolio and investment plans against each scenario. For each scenario the Group analyzes its main impacts (e.g. operational resilience, strategic implications, economic impact). The assessment is based on quantification of every sector's reduction pathway (e.g. refining, distribution etc.).

The scenarios analysis involves evaluating different Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) scenarios. The quantitative analysis of the risks and opportunities, climatic and economic data are those derived by scenarios developed by IPCC Assessment reports AR54 and AR65.

Scenario of Weak Policies: Climate mitigation actions are delayed, thus the GHG emissions continue to rise throughout the 21st century and the global mean temperature increases above 3℃ at the end of the century. Climate risks and opportunities are mainly related to physical impacts as both the chronic and acute climate change effects are significant.

In this scenario, the Group evaluates the potential consequences of unchecked emissions growth, including more severe climate-related events, stricter regulatory measures and increased pressure from stakeholders to decarbonize. Within this framework, the Group assesses how extreme weather events, rising sea levels and other climate-related risks associated with RCP 8.5 could affect infrastructures, supply chain resilience and long-term business viability. This leads to a better understanding of potential future range outcomes and to strategies that mitigate risks, capitalize on opportunities and contribute to global efforts to address climate change. This analysis affects decision-making processes, guiding investments, operations and sustainability initiatives to align with the most likely future scenarios while maintaining resilience in the face of uncertainty. In addition, thorough assessments of various policy action scenarios are conducted to understand their potential impact on climate goals and business operations.

Scenario of Current Policies: Transition to a lower carbon future is in line with the Paris Agreement. Climate mitigation policies are stronger compared to the previous scenario and lead to global mean temperature increases between 2℃ and 3℃ at the end of the century.

In this scenario, the potential implications of regulatory measures, technological advancements and product demand aimed at reducing carbon emissions are considered. The Group assesses also, how a trajectory consistent with RCP 4.5 could impact its operations, including potential changes in energy demand, regulatory compliance requirements and investment risks and opportunities in low-carbon technologies.

Net Zero scenario: Incorporation of strong climate mitigation policies and shows a pathway for the global energy sector to achieve net zero CO2 emissions by 2050, consistent with limiting the global temperature rise to 1.5- 2.0℃ up to the end of this century with no or limited temperature overshoot.

In this scenario, MOTOR OIL Group evaluates the implications of emission reduction targets, accelerated deployment of renewable energy sources and rapid transition to low-carbon technologies. The critical point of this assessment is when the policies will be launched. This issue leads to challenges as well as to opportunities which require significant investments in clean energy infrastructure while positioning it as a leader in sustainable energy solutions:

Late Policy Action - Limited to 1.5℃ but with High Cost: Delayed policy intervention leads to the need for more drastic and costly measures to achieve the 1.5℃ warming limit. The MOTOR OIL Group assesses the potential financial impacts of delayed regulatory actions, including higher compliance costs and increased pressure from stakeholders to accelerate decarbonization efforts. Late policy action underscores the importance of proactive risk management and strategic planning to mitigate financial risks and ensure the Group's resilience in a rapidly changing regulatory landscape.

No Additional Policy Action - Limited to 2℃ with Stronger Policies Needed: Limited additional policy action beyond existing commitments, results to global warming limited to 2℃ above pre-industrial levels. The MOTOR OIL Group analyzes the implications of insufficient regulatory measures, such as increased climate-related risks and market uncertainty. While current policies may fall short of the 1.5℃ target, the Group identifies opportunities to advocate for stronger climate policies, invest in resilience measures and innovate to reduce emissions and mitigate climate risks. By evaluating these policy action scenarios, the Group gains valuable insights into potential regulatory developments and their impact on the energy sector. This analysis informs strategic decision-making, enabling the Group to adapt to evolving climate policies and maintain its long-term competitiveness while contributing to global efforts to combat climate change.

The MOTOR OIL Group reduction plan as described in the Group's "Energy Transition Strategy and Outlook Targets 2030", has been quantitatively included in the reduction scenarios tool.

The main scenarios analysis and results focused on the three basic scenarios described (WP, CP, NZ) and additionally a NZ scenario where the CCS project would not be implemented before 2030.

The Group's reduction plan as outlined in the "Energy Transition Strategy and Outlook Targets 2030" could potentially reduce emission derived from scope 1&2, up to 770 ktCO2e per year. Under Current Policies (CP) and NZ Scenarios it would very likely that the scope 1&2 targets could be reached. In any case fulfilment of scope 1&2 target is highly dependent on the implementation of the CCS project and related actions, as these would cover the majority (~70%) of the emissions impacts for Scope 1&2. The market outlook predictions for NZ scenario are based on data from IEA for the global energy demand and global oil demand and National Energy and Climate plan for the Greek market outlook of NG. However, the IEA predictions for the European energy and European oil demand differ significantly to the global outlook. If future policies and regulations steer MOTOR OIL Group toward a trajectory aligned with the European market outlook - where global oil demand is projected to decline by approximately 25% (compared to a 5% reduction in international markets) - the resulting decrease in Scope 3 emissions could enable the Group to achieve its current Scope 3 emissions reduction target of -25% by 2030.

<!-- Report Page 170 -->
##### Resilience of the Group's strategy

The Group has already developed a multi-dimensional climate strategy that allows for a smooth adaptation to stricter regulations and carbon pricing mechanisms, reduction of financial risk and ensuring long-term resilience.

The Resilience Strategy is based on the above climate policy scenarios and includes the following key pillars:

· a diversified energy portfolio with expansion into renewable energy sources, investments in

· natural gas, biofuels and hydrogen and development of electromobility infrastructure.. financial and operational resilience through revenue diversification beyond the refining sector,. carbon capture initiatives, digitization investments, improvements in the supply chain and infrastructure.. adaptation to Regulations and the Market through compliance with EU climate policies and. active participation in sustainability frameworks.. adopting measurable GHG reduction and decarbonization goals and

· formulating a climate transition plan aligned with the 1.5℃ target.

##### Time Horizons of Analysis

MOTOR OIL Group employs scenario analysis across three defined time horizons to systematically assess climate-related risks and opportunities, ensuring strategic resilience in an increasingly dynamic energy environment.

Short-term (1-5 years): This period marks a phase of rapid transformation driven by geopolitical developments, evolving policy frameworks and heightened energy security concerns. The Group's focus is on accelerating investments in low-carbon and renewable energy projects, while actively managing operational and financial risks amid market volatility.

Medium-term (5-15 years): During this phase, the Group aims to scale up green initiatives and embed them across its operations. Emphasis is placed on the expansion of clean technologies, advancement in green hydrogen production and deployment of carbon capture solutions, all while adapting to shifting global trade dynamics and increasingly stringent regulatory landscapes.

Long-term (15-25 years): Representing the full realization of the Group's strategic transition, this horizon focuses on achieving net-zero emissions and becoming a leader in sustainable energy solutions. Key priorities include value chain-wide decarbonization, integration of circular economy principles and adoption of sustainable financing tools to secure MOTOR OIL Group's role in shaping the future energy system.

Further insights into the implications of these time horizons for strategic planning can be found in the chapter titled "Transition Plan for Climate Change" within this report.

<!-- Report Page 172 -->
#### 3.2 Impact, risk and opportunity management [E1.SBM-3]

##### 3.2.1 Policies and actions related to Climate Change Adaptation [E1-2] [E1-3]

MOTOR OIL Group has developed a structured framework to address material impacts, risks and opportunities related to climate change adaptation. This framework is embedded within the Group's Environmental Policy and broader Sustainability Policy, ensuring that climate resilience is integrated into all aspects of operations and strategic planning. The Group's Climate Change Adaptation Strategy outlines a systematic approach to assessing and mitigating climate-related risks. This includes identifying vulnerabilities within infrastructure and operational processes to determine necessary adaptation measures.

MOTOR OIL Group's adaptation policies account for a wide range of climate-related physical risks, including acute events such as hurricanes, floods and extreme heat that can disrupt operations and damage infrastructure, as well as chronic risks like rising sea levels and temperature shifts, which may impact assets and supply chains over the long term. These risks are integrated into the Group's investment planning and risk management processes to minimize financial and operational exposure.

To strengthen resilience, the Group applies stringent engineering and design standards to new infrastructure, while existing assets undergo climate risk assessments to identify and implement necessary upgrades. Site selection and land-use policies prioritize areas with lower climate vulnerability, supported by contingency planning for high-risk zones.

In its renewable energy operations, the Group mitigates physical risks through resilient infrastructure design, careful land-use planning and targeted adaptation measures. Collaboration with government bodies, local communities and industry partners ensures coordinated risk management and collective resilience-building.

Furthermore, the Group incorporates climate scenario analysis into project planning to proactively identify and address potential climate-related impacts. These insights are regularly communicated to stakeholders, ensuring transparency on how climate risks and opportunities influence strategic and operational decisions.

Operational adaptation measures include modifications in maintenance schedules, upgrades to critical infrastructure and the deployment of early warning systems for extreme weather events. The Group implements emergency response protocols and business continuity plans to minimize disruption in the event of climate-related incidents. Supply chain policies incorporate risk assessments to address potential disruptions caused by climate hazards, ensuring continued operational stability.

As will be further detailed, MOTOR OIL Group's policies also focus on energy efficiency and emissions reduction as part of its adaptation efforts. Investments in process optimization, digital monitoring systems and lower-carbon technologies contribute to overall climate resilience. Operational policies promote the use of climate scenario analyses to anticipate long-term regulatory, market and physical climate risks. All business activities are certified under ISO 14001:2015, a framework for implementing adaptation measures across all business units. Environmental risk assessments, conducted in accordance with certification requirements, help the Group align its climate policies with best practices. Compliance with national and international climate adaptation frameworks is monitored to ensure continuous alignment with evolving regulatory standards.

As part of its climate adaptation strategy, MOTOR OIL Group has implemented the "Forest Program", a nature-based initiative focused on preserving biodiversity, restoring degraded ecosystems and enhancing climate resilience.

The program operates through three pillars: seed collection and conservation; large-scale plant cultivation through forestry nurseries, with a combined potential of over 3.5 million plants; and targeted reforestation projects across 52 hectares of fire-affected areas in Attica. Beyond reforestation, the program supports research, innovation and long-term environmental monitoring, with a carbon mitigation potential of approximately 208 tCO2 - demonstrating the Group's commitment to ecosystem restoration and sustainable land use.

Physical risks are also intertwined with transition risks, particularly for the Group's renewable energy activities. The reliability and efficiency of renewable energy infrastructure may be impacted by extreme weather events, temperature fluctuations, water availability and resource variability. Coastal renewable energy projects, such as offshore wind farms, face additional risks due to rising sea levels and coastal erosion. To mitigate these risks, the Group incorporates climate risk assessments and scenario analysis into project planning, collaborates with stakeholders to enhance resilience and adopts robust design and land-use strategies to safeguard infrastructure.

The Group is addressing the transition risks through strategic investments in renewable energy projects and alternative fuels, recognizing both the risks and opportunities presented by the transition. More specifically transition risk analysis highlighted the need for accelerated diversification into biofuels, green hydrogen and electrification, as well as resilience against carbon pricing above €100/ton through efficiency and circular economy strategies. By expanding into clean energy sectors, the Group aligns with decarbonization efforts while enhancing its market appeal to environmentally conscious investors.

As a result of the resilience and scenario analysis, MOTOR OIL Group is accelerating its energy transition strategy by reallocating significant CAPEX toward green technologies and climate-resilient infrastructure, updating business continuity and risk management frameworks with site-specific adaptation measures and reinforcing its net-zero by 2050 ambition and multi-energy growth trajectory. The analysis demonstrates the Group's ability to adapt its strategy and business model over the short-, medium- and long-term by shifting its product and service portfolio toward low-carbon and renewable solutions, securing continued access to finance at an affordable cost of capital, redeploying and upgrading existing assets, planning the decommissioning of carbon-intensive infrastructure and investing in the accelerated diversification of its operations - particularly in biofuels, green hydrogen and the electrification of energy services - while also reskilling its workforce to support and sustain this transformation.

Participation in EU programs focused on alternative fuel technologies, as well as investments in mature technologies such as RES and EV chargers, further strengthen the Group's positioning within the evolving energy landscape.

Engagement with stakeholders ensures transparency and awareness of mitigation efforts, while policies are made publicly available to all potentially affected parties through the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) plan. The Group actively communicates its environmental and climate-related policies via public consultation processes, regulatory filings and stakeholder forums, ensuring accessibility for communities, regulatory bodies and partners. Additionally, stakeholders who play a role in policy implementation are engaged through targeted meetings, training sessions and collaborative initiatives to ensure alignment and effective execution of the Group's sustainability objectives.

Climate risk oversight is managed by the Sustainability Committee, which includes members of the Board of Directors and senior management. The Committee is responsible for evaluating adaptation strategies, monitoring policy implementation and ensuring that climate-related risks are addressed through structured mitigation measures. CapEx/OpEx in decarbonization actions referenced in taxonomy chapter and also E1-1.

<!-- Report Page 174 -->
##### 3.2.2 Targets related to Climate Change Adaptation [E1-4]

MOTOR OIL Group recognizes the critical importance of climate change adaptation in ensuring the long-term resilience of its operations, supply chains and the communities in which it operates. The Group acknowledges that adapting to climate-related risks, including extreme weather events and shifting environmental conditions, is essential for sustainable business continuity. While adaptation remains a key consideration in the Group's broader sustainability strategy, specific quantitative targets for climate change adaptation have not yet been formally established. MOTOR OIL Group has conducted a climate resilience analysis, but its outcome has not been fully evaluated yet in order to set specific targets. MOTOR OIL Group continues to assess the evolving regulatory landscape, industry best practices and scientific advancements to integrate adaptation measures effectively. The Group remains committed to enhancing climate resilience and will continue to evaluate and refine its approach in alignment with global frameworks and stakeholder expectations.

Adaptation to physical climate risks is closely linked to mitigation efforts, as reducing emissions and improving energy efficiency lower CO2 concentrations, ultimately decreasing climate-related risks. To enhance climate resilience, MOTOR OIL Group has implemented a comprehensive adaptation strategy across its operations, infrastructure and supply chains. The Group integrates climate risk assessments into planning, reinforces infrastructure to withstand extreme weather and prioritizes low-risk site selection. It has also developed business continuity plans and supply chain risk assessments to minimize climate disruptions. Additionally, it invests in green technologies, closed-loop water systems and biodiversity initiatives, such as the Forest Program.

Alongside these adaptation measures, MOTOR OIL Group has set key environmental targets, reviewed and updated annually, including:

· Water Management: Reducing water consumption and improving efficiency.

· Waste Management: Minimizing waste generation and promoting circular economy practices.

##### 3.2.3 Policies and actions related to Climate Change Mitigation [E1-2] [E1-3]

MOTOR OIL Group has developed and implements a climate change mitigation strategy aimed at reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, transitioning to low-carbon energy sources and aligning its operations with global decarbonization targets. The Group's mitigation approach is embedded within its broader Environmental and Sustainability Policies and it's implemented through internationally recognized standards, including ISO 14001 and ISO 50001 while follows the respected climate related laws.

Key Mitigation Policies and Actions:. Net-Zero Commitment: The Group is committed to achieving net-zero emissions by 2050, supported by interim targets including a 30% reduction in Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions and 25% in Scope 3 emissions by 2030.

· Investments in Renewables: Strategic investments have been made in solar and wind power projects, alongside alternative fuel technologies, including biofuels and green hydrogen, primarily within the Refining and Power & Gas sectors.. Energy Efficiency & Process Optimization: Operational improvements include digital monitoring systems, energy efficiency enhancements, "green fuels" deployment and lower-carbon technologies aimed at reducing emissions intensity.. Carbon Capture and Storage (CCS): Consideration is being given to CCS technologies as part of long-term mitigation strategies.. Onsite Renewable Solutions & RECs: The Group is actively exploring onsite renewable installations and the purchase of Renewable Energy Certificates (RECs) to lower emissions and support clean energy production.. Stakeholder Alignment: Engagement with stakeholders ensures transparency and awareness of mitigation efforts, while policies are made publicly available to all potentially affected parties through the Environmental Impact Assessment plan.. Participation in EU Programs: The Group takes part in European alternative fuel initiatives and sustainable finance programs, enhancing its credibility and positioning in the evolving low-carbon economy.

Please refer to table titled "Estimated emissions reduction(tCO,e)-All Scenarios". Please note that naturebased solutions have not been implemented as part of MOTOR OIL Group's climate change mitigation strategy yet.

##### 3.2.4 Targets related to Climate Change Mitigation [E1-4]

In setting its climate change mitigation targets, MOTOR OIL Group considered a comprehensive set of drivers reflecting both market dynamics and environmental imperatives. Key inputs included projections for energy demand, aligned with expected production volumes and long-term growth patterns, as well as shifts in oil and natural gas demand under various transition scenarios.

The increasing pace of electrification, driven by sustainable mobility (e.g., electric vehicle adoption) and the broader decarbonization of end-users - from residential to industrial sectors - was also factored in, alongside growing demand for renewable energy sources (RES) and the transformation of the electricity grid. The Group evaluated trends in biofuel and bioenergy consumption, as well as the emerging role of low-carbon hydrogen, recognizing their potential to support emissions reduction across hard-toabate sectors. In addition, technological advancements, evolving climate policies and carbon pricing mechanisms were critical drivers influencing target setting. The analysis also incorporated geopolitical developments affecting energy security and the increasing prevalence of extreme weather events, which underscored the urgency of addressing climate-related physical risks. Together, these factors shaped a science- and scenario-informed mitigation pathway aligned with the Group's long-term sustainability and resilience objectives.

In developing its climate change mitigation targets, MOTOR OIL Group engaged proactively with key stakeholders to ensure alignment with its internal sustainability goals and the expectations of the wider external environment. This process involved dialogue with investors, regulatory authorities, customers, employees, industry partners and local communities - either directly or through their representatives - whose insights contributed to shaping a climate strategy that is scientifically grounded. More specifically The Group's mitigation targets related to environmental matters are grounded in conclusive scientific evidence and aligned with internationally recognized frameworks such as the Paris Agreement, the European Green Deal and the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These targets are informed by climate scenario analyses, sectoral decarbonization pathways and data from authoritative sources including the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) and the International Energy Agency (IEA).

A key target within its decarbonization strategy is to achieve a 30% reduction in Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions by 2030, using 2021 as the baseline year This 30% reduction will come as a result of approximately 1% reduction in GHG Scope 2 emissions and the rest (approx. 29%) in GHG Scope 1 emissions. This target reflects the Group's dedication to optimizing its operations, enhancing energy efficiency and integrating cleaner energy sources into its production processes. At the end of reporting year, 1.26% of reduction is achieved. For further details, please refer below to "Total GHG Emissions Progress against Targets" table. Through investments in advanced technologies, electrification and energy efficiency improvements, MOTOR OIL Group aims to significantly lower direct emissions from its refining, power and gas operations. Additionally, the Group is actively incorporating renewable energy solutions into its facilities and securing Renewable Energy Certificates (RECs) to support its transition to a low-carbon business model.

<!-- Report Page 176 -->
On a high-level approach, each initiative's contribution in scope 1 & 2 is presented below:

The above figure presents the measures to be implemented in refinery against the target, which is the major contributor for scope 1 & 2 emissions. More info about the total baseline emissions and reporting year's progress are presented in E1-6.

Beyond direct emissions, MOTOR OIL Group recognizes the importance of addressing value chain emissions and has set a target to reduce Scope 3 emissions by 25% using 2021 as the baseline year. At the end of reporting year, -3.78% of reduction is achieved. For further details, please refer below to "Total GHG Emissions Progress against Targets" table. This objective reflects the Group's commitment to influencing and collaborating with its suppliers, partners and customers to drive systemic change across the industry. A key focus is on promoting lower-carbon products, expanding alternative fuel offerings and enhancing the efficiency of distribution networks. Additionally, the Group engages with its stakeholders to advance responsible sourcing practices and encourage carbon reduction strategies across the supply chain

MOTOR OIL Group actively involves its stakeholders in the process of setting climate mitigation targets through structured engagement activities, including surveys, bilateral meetings and dedicated focus groups. Baseline values against which the progress is measured is representative of the high emitting activities of MOTOR OIL Group, including refining and retail section.

This inclusive approach ensures that the Group's decarbonization goals - such as reducing Scope 1, 2 and 3 emissions by 2030 and achieving net-zero by 2050 - are aligned with stakeholder expectations, industry best practices and regulatory developments. Input from investors, employees, regulators, communities and supply chain partners helps shape realistic, impactful targets and fosters a sense of shared responsibility in advancing the Group's climate strategy.
