##### 3.2.5 Policies and actions related to Energy [E1-2] [E1-3]

MOTOR OIL Group places energy efficiency at the heart of its sustainability and climate strategy, recognizing its critical role in reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, conserving natural resources and supporting the global transition to a low-carbon economy. Given the energy-intensive nature of the Group's operations - particularly in refining - responsible and sustainable energy use is a strategic priority.

The Group's management plays an active and leading role in driving energy performance initiatives, ensuring full alignment with the Energy and Environmental Policies, which are embedded within the broader Sustainability Policy. These policies form the foundation for the Group's Energy Strategy, which is structured around three core pillars:

1\. Energy Efficiency and Optimization: All Groups' energy intensive activities operate under an Energy Management System certified with ISO 50001, which enables the systematic monitoring, evaluation and optimization of energy consumption across all operational areas. This includes setting measurable energy performance targets, identifying areas for improvement and integrating best available technologies to enhance efficiency.

2\. Renewable Energy and Low-Carbon Transition: The Group is committed to expanding its renewable energy portfolio, investing in wind, solar and alternative fuel projects to reduce its reliance on fossil fuels.

3\. Sustainable Energy Practices in Operations and Supply Chain: The Group actively encourages suppliers, business partners and regulatory bodies to promote sustainable energy practices across its value chain. By leveraging digital transformation and smart energy solutions, the Group ensures efficient resource utilization while minimizing energy-related risks.

<!-- Report Page 178 -->
## Energy Management System (EnMS)

Central to the Group's energy performance is its Energy Management System, which governs the efficient use of energy across all facilities, with particular focus on refining operations. The EnMS is grounded in the principles of continuous improvement, enabling systematic monitoring, analysis and optimization of energy usage, while ensuring ongoing compliance with legal requirements and energy performance standards. As part of this commitment, the Group:

· Establishes and regularly reviews energy-related objectives and targets;. Allocates the necessary resources and data to support improvement initiatives;. Encourages the adoption of best practices and integration of technological advancements to enhance operational efficiency.

Procurement practices are aligned with energy performance goals, prioritizing energy-efficient products and services throughout all phases of operations - from design and construction to production and maintenance. Standardized practices across all facilities ensure consistent, measurable improvements in performance. The Group's Energy Management Policy reflects its dedication to Preserving natural resources, reducing greenhouse gas emissions and contributing to climate change mitigation through the responsible and efficient use of energy.

### 3.2.6 Targets related to Energy [E1-4]

As part of its commitment to energy transition and a more sustainable future, MOTOR OIL Group is continuously expanding its renewable energy portfolio. The Group has set a target to achieve a renewable energy capacity of 2 GW by 2030, reinforcing its role in the development of clean energy solutions. Through strategic investments in solar, wind and other renewable technologies, the Group aims to diversify its energy mix, reduce its carbon footprint and contribute to national and international climate goals.

In parallel, MOTOR OIL Group is advancing its biofuel production program, targeting 250 ktpa (kilotons per annum) as part of its ongoing efforts to provide sustainable fuel alternatives. By leveraging advanced biofuel technologies, the Group is actively reducing reliance on conventional fossil fuels while supporting the transportation sector's shift toward lower-carbon solutions. The integration of biofuels into the energy mix aligns with both regulatory frameworks and market demand for cleaner mobility options.

A key component of the Group's low-carbon strategy is the development of low-carbon hydrogen production, with a target of 60 ktpa by 2029. Hydrogen is expected to play a crucial role in the decarbonization of industrial processes and heavy transport, offering a viable pathway toward emissions reduction. MOTOR OIL Group is investing in cutting-edge technologies to produce hydrogen efficiently and sustainably, ensuring its role as a leading player in the emerging hydrogen economy.

Further expanding its portfolio of alternative fuels, the Group is actively investing in e-methanol production, targeting 25 ktpa by 2029. E-methanol, produced using renewable energy sources and carbon capture technologies, represents a key solution for reducing emissions in the maritime and industrial sectors. Additionally, the Group is strengthening its biodiesel production from biogenic feedstock, with a target of 200 ktpa, as part of its ongoing commitment to circular economy principles. By utilizing sustainable feedstocks such as waste oils and agricultural residues, the Group is enhancing resource efficiency while contributing to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. These efforts support the transition toward a more sustainable fuels market, ensuring compliance with evolving environmental regulations and reinforcing MOTOR OIL Group's leadership in responsible energy production.

###### Methodological approach

The Group's energy consumption framework is structured around multiple sources, considering national reporting standards and applicable regional data.

The total energy entering and consumed in the refinery is derived from three primary sources: (i) the import and consumption of fuels - either purchased or self-produced - classified as primary energy, (ii) electricity introduced from the national grid, converted into primary energy and (iii) fuel consumption for the Group's vehicle operations. For subsidiaries operating in Greece, energy consumption from nuclear sources is considered zero, in accordance with the National Inventory Report (Edition 2024). For subsidiaries in Cyprus, Bulgaria, Serbia, North Macedonia, Croatia and Romania, nuclear energy consumption is calculated using relevant and applicable data.

<!-- Report Page 180 -->
Refinery utilizes various fuel types, including:

· Self-generated fuel oil.

· Refinery fuel gas as mixture of self-generated fuel gas, LPG and natural gas.. Natural Gas as imported fuel source.

Energy consumption metrics of significant activities are externally validated every year in the context of ISO 50001:2018. For the reporting year, validation process is not yet completed.

MOTOR OIL refinery's energy consumption is also externally validated in the context of the EU ETS.

A portion of the fuel and imported electricity is further utilized for the internal generation of electricity, steam and compressed air, which support industrial processes and operational needs. Additionally, self-generated non-fuel renewable energy originates from renewable electricity production at CORAL's facilities, contributing to the Group's energy mix.

###### Methodological approach

MOTOR OIL's refinery CO2 emissions calculations adhere to the EU-ETS methodology and are validated from external third party, as outlined in Regulation 2018/2066, in accordance with Directive 2003/87/EC and its subsequent amendments. Emission monitoring is conducted based on an approved Monitoring Plan, which details the applied methodology and is fully documented in a standardized electronic template published by EU CLIMA.

The Monitoring Plan includes:. Compliance with uncertainty limits for the applied tiers, with uncertainty calculations for each source stream documented in the EU CLIMA uncertainty tool (Annex 4).. A risk assessment framework designed to ensure control over emission monitoring procedures, documented in the EU CLIMA risk assessment tool.

For other Group installations and additional GHG gases, calculations align with methodologies, tools and fuel property data provided by the Hellenic Ministry of Environment and Energy, ensuring compliance with National Climate Law reporting obligations.

GHG emissions regulated by the EU ETS and total GHG emissions reported on this statement refer to the same reporting period.

Regarding Scope 2 GHG emissions:. Location-based emissions are calculated using the latest Emission Factors (EF) provided by the National Inventory Report (Edition 2024) for installations and subsidiaries in Greece, while countryspecific EF are applied for operations in Cyprus, Bulgaria, Serbia, North Macedonia, Croatia and Romania.. Market-based emissions for Greek operations utilize EF data from the latest DAPEEP publication. Electricity for Group installations was primarily supplied by NRG, with PPC covering the remainder. Where multiple providers were used, emissions were calculated using a weighted mean approach.

The quantification of the other indirect emissions (Scope 3) of greenhouse gases (GHG) of the MOTOR OIL Group is carried out in accordance with the internationally recognized GHG Protocol and the ISO 14064-1:2018 standard, also taking into account the guidelines of the document "Estimating petroleum industry value chain (Scope 3) greenhouse gas emissions." Overview of methodologies" of IPIECA and API. The primary data for the corporate activities concerning Scope 3 emissions are collected for all the Group companies included in the ATH inventory and, if necessary, are processed to obtain the appropriate format (e.g. unit conversions). Then, the GHG emissions associated with each activity / emission source are calculated, applying appropriate emission factors from reliable sources.

Indicatively, the following is a list of sources of emission factors used during the GHG inventory (Scope
Inventory Report, IEA.

GHG accounting for MOTOR OIL Group's total emissions was held following GHG Protocol's guidance and most recent GWP values were used according to Sixth Assessment of IPCC (AR6).

Also, GHG emissions accounting boundaries include the parent company and its subsidiaries, as well as joint ventures and associates on which MOTOR OIL has operational control.

Lastly, no guarantees of origin were acquired during the reporting period.

##### 3.3.3 GHG removals and GHG mitigation projects financed through carbon credits [E1-7]

###### Carbon removals and Carbon Credits

During the reporting period, MOTOR OIL Group did not engage in any GHG removal activities, nor did it finance any GHG mitigation projects through the use of carbon credits. Also, no carbon credits were purchased, sold, or transferred by MOTOR OIL Group during the reporting period.

Also, MOTOR OIL Group does not store any tonnes of CO2 derived from carbon removal projects implemented within its own operations or across the upstream or downstream stages of its value chain.

However, as part of its commitment to achieving net-zero emissions across Scope 1, 2 and 3 and in line with the National Climate Law (including the target of a 30% reduction in Scope 1 & 2 emissions), the Group acknowledges that, should its targets not be fully met through decarbonization technologies within its energy production processes, it may pursue offsetting strategies.

These would include the use of the Group's own Natural Based Solutions (NBS) projects or highintegrity carbon credits, aligned with internationally recognized frameworks such as Article 6 of the Paris Agreement, the EU Carbon Removal Certification Framework (CRCF), or the National Voluntary Carbon Framework (once available) and any credits used would ensure transparency, additionality, permanence and methodological rigor.

# 4 POLLUTION [E2]

<!-- Report Page 186 -->
## 4.1 Impact, risk and opportunity management

### 4.1.1 Policies and Actions related to Pollution [E2-1] [E2-2]

MOTOR OIL Group has adopted and implements an Environmental Policy that includes environmental provisions, obligatory for all employees and covering own operations, under the scope of ISO 14001 certification schemes. The Policy seeks to promote environmental sensitivity and accountability, both by Management and employees and requires not only compliance with applicable legislation but, where possible, go beyond compliance requirements. The framework was established to manage and control pollution, aims to minimize emissions to air, water and soil, ensuring compliance with environmental regulations in the regions where the Group operates. It also aims to mitigate negative impacts related to air and water pollution. Pollutants under scope are defined on each installation's Environmental Terms. This framework forms an integral part of the Group's Sustainability Policy where environmental protection is fundamental factor for a long-term, responsible and successful business strategy. With respect to Environment, MOTOR OIL Group is committed to:. Set objectives, which are compatible with its strategic orientation and accomplish a continuous improvement of its environmental performance.. Follow industry trends and adopt new technology for the optimization of its activities.. Meet or exceed legal and other requirements in respect to its assets and society's needs.. Make all efforts to minimize its environmental impact, including pollution prevention, improving the quality and the treatment of waste, discharges and emissions.. Report its performance, good or bad, in the context of corporate social responsibility.

· Integrate Environmental considerations into all business decisions, plans, processes and operations in the framework of the Integrated Management System.. Provide consultation, information and training to employees, contractors and suppliers working to ensure their commitment to environmental protection and awareness.

Sustainability Committee is responsible for the supervision of the implementation of environmental management and ensures that measures to control emissions are effectively integrated into daily operations. The development and periodic review of the policy takes into consideration the interests of key stakeholders, including regulatory authorities, local communities and industry partners to promote transparency and accountability. The Policy is publicly available on Motor Oil's website and through annual Environmental Statements.

MOTOR OIL Group publicly discloses its environmental management performance, beyond Sustainability Statement, through annual reports to competent authorities and EMAS environmental statements.

### Air Pollution: Refinery activities

MOTOR OIL Group is committed to compliance with all applicable environmental laws and regulations, ensuring the effective monitoring, control and reduction of emissions across its industrial operations. It has established Key Performance Indicators to track the effectiveness of its air pollution control policies and ensure alignment with regulatory requirements and best practices. The targets focus on emission reductions, pollution prevention and resource management to minimize environmental impact.

MOTOR OIL Group aiming at improving air quality according to its environmental policy and the latest Environmental Permit, implements where available the Best Available Techniques (BATs) during the operation of its facilities that directly contribute to the control of air pollutants and their specific loads. During the reporting period, usual maintenance actions took place when it comes to the equipment used, therefore not contributing substantially to Group's OpEx or CapEx.

The application of BAT (Implementing Decision 2014/738/EU) related to air pollution at the refinery includes measures to prevent or reduce emissions during the facilities operation:. Incorporation of BATs either into the design of a new unit or when existing units and processes are expanded or modified.. Electrostatic filter (ESP) operation at the Fluid Catalytic Cracking Unit (FCCU), intended to reduce emissions of suspended particles deriving from catalyst.

· Reduction and control of hydrocarbon fugitive emissions by efficient measures and equipment, such as closed circuits in gas processing operations, routing of gases from safety valves to flares, secondary seals in floating roof tanks, floating covers in oil separators and a Vapor Recovery Unit (VRU) in the truck loading terminal. Additionally, a new vapor recovery unit, at the port facility area, is currently at the design phase.. Maximization of natural gas usage, in the refinery fuel mixture.. Automations and control systems that contribute to the efficient management of raw materials and energy, while supporting to maintain high levels of reliability and safety. Performance control of furnaces and boilers.. Continuous monitoring of main pollutants (SO2, NOX, PM, CO) in large combustion plants including power plant and the fluid catalytic cracking unit and periodic monitoring of main pollutants (SO2, NOx, PM, CO) in every other stack of the refinery by an accredited third party.. Implementation of a leak detection and repair program (LDAR) to limit fugitive emissions from the equipment, using both sniffing and optical methods.

Integrated sulfur management system: The challenge refineries face is that the amount of sulfur in the available crude oil types increases over time, while the specifications for allowable sulfur content in the fuels produced have been reduced. To counteract this challenge and through its Hydrocracker Complex, MOTOR OIL delivers petrol and automotive diesel with a sulfur content below 10 ppm, whilst minimizing sulfur emissions. Moreover, the maximum acceptable level of sulfur emissions is low (<600 kg/h) and the air quality concentration of sulfur dioxide, on an hourly basis, should not exceed 350 ug/m3. In this context, the removal of sulfur coming into the refinery with crude oil -at a concentration which in 2024 ranged between 2.1% and 2.8%- is a significant challenge. The challenge is addressed by desulfurization of gases used as fuel in the refinery before they enter the gas fuel system, reducing emissions of sulfur dioxide and the operation of sulfur recovery units for converting hydrogen sulfide deriving from process into solid sulfur. Elemental sulfur is initially produced in liquid form, which by cooling and appropriate processing, is converted to a solid granular form, stored in silos for sale as a raw material.

To maintain high environmental standards, Refinery operates Air Quality Monitoring Network consisting of a dedicated monitoring station near its port premises, measuring a wide range of pollutants, including hydrogen sulfide (H2S), sulfur dioxide (SO2), particulate matter (PM10, PM2.5), nitrogen oxides (NO, NO2, NOx), methane (CH4), non-methane hydrocarbons (NMHC), total hydrocarbons (THC), benzene (C,H2) and carbon monoxide (CO). The network also records key meteorological parameters such as wind direction and speed, air temperature and relative humidity. Additionally, three dedicated measuring stations specifically track hydrogen sulfide (H,S) and sulfur dioxide (SO2), with two positioned around the refinery's perimeter and one located in Agioi Theodoroi, ensuring comprehensive air quality oversight.

Finally, to align with a new global cap on marine fuel oils that came in force since start of 2020, having as a scope to reduce the air pollution coming from the shipping industry, refinery adapted its operational parameter and crude diet to produce this low sulfur fuel oil (0.5%) since the beginning of 2021.

<!-- Report Page 188 -->
### Water Pollution: Refinery activities

Due to nature of activities, there are not specific targets for effluent discharges from operational sites. The deployment of BAT Techniques and ISO 14001 management system support the legal compliance with subjected environmental terms.

As part of its broader Sustainability Policy, the Group's water pollution prevention strategy incorporates wastewater treatment, operational controls and preventive maintenance programs to minimize environmental impact while maintaining industrial efficiency. These measures apply across all operating sites when pollutants direct to seawater or municipal sewages.

A core component of MOTOR OIL Group's environmental management is the effective treatment of wastewater generated during refinery operations. Due to their different pollutant load, the industrial and sanitary wastewater streams undergo different types of processing prior to their final disposal. For the industrial waste, the treatment process consists of advanced treatment technologies, including effective treatment stages (API type oil separators, Dissolved Air Flotation (DAF), sand filters, biofilters, sludge treatment) incorporating the Best Available Techniques. The effective operation of the Industrial Wastewater Treatment Plant is evidenced by the fact that concentrations of various pollutant at the plant outlet are below relevant limit values as defined by Environmental Terms which they follow BAT -AEL limits (Implementing Decision 2014/738/EU). As a rule, depending on its origin and the nature of the contaminants present (e.g. sulfur compounds, phenols, alkalinity, ammonia), wastewater are pretreated in intermediate units or directly in the refinery's Industrial Wastewater Treatment Plant. In terms of intermediate treatment, the main three units responsible for processing of wastewater are the Sour Water Stripper unit, the Spent Caustic Neutralisation unit and the Neutralisation unit treating acidic effluents from the alkylation unit.

The sanitary waste treatment process consists of three treatment steps:

· Primary treatment (screening, precipitation, flow balancing). Secondary treatment (biological active sludge treatment, clarification)

· Tertiary treatment (nitrification/denitrification, chlorination)

Routine assessments and audits ensure that wastewater treatment processes remain efficient and compliant with environmental standards. To further mitigate water contamination risks, the Group enforces operational controls at all industrial sites. Sealed drainage systems and containment basins are in place to prevent accidental spills from entering local water bodies.

Preventive maintenance is another key element of MOTOR OIL Group's strategy. The company conducts regular inspections of pipelines, storage tanks and wastewater treatment units to identify and resolve potential failures before they result in environmental damage.

### Water Pollution: Retail activities

At CORAL, a water management approach was implemented, redesigning the wastewater drainage system at the Ag. Theodoroi oil terminal to separate stormwater from wastewater before treatment. This initiative prevents contamination of local water sources, directly addressing community concerns over industrial effluents.

### Circular Economy & Alternative Fuels activities

PRASINO LADI is dedicated to minimizing its environmental footprint and promoting sustainability throughout its operations. One of key initiatives is the collection and responsible management of used cooking oils. PRASINO LADI understands the detrimental effects that improper disposal of these oils can have on the environment.

To mitigate impacts caused in terms of pollution, PRASINO LADI diligently collects used cooking oils and forward them to VERD for proper processing and recycling. By doing so, it doesn't only prevent the oils from harming the environment but also contribute to the production of biodiesel. Biodiesel is a renewable and cleaner alternative to conventional fuels, helping to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and reliance on fossil fuels.

In VERD production plant there is thorough monitoring of air emissions from the burners daily, to support meeting compliance with environmental terms. In one of the two steam producing burners, the fuel is a production by-product (Bio-Heating Agent (BHA)) which is a low sulfur (<0.1%) fuel while its carbon footprint is very low, minimizing the use of fossil fuel oil and the subsequent effect on the emissions. Furthermore, scrubber systems prevent methanol emissions, while biofilters manage odors resulting from the processing of used vegetable oils and animal fats. These integrated measures reflect a holistic approach to environmental stewardship.

As part of the Group's circular economy and alternative fuels activities, measures have been implemented to minimize environmental impact during production. These initiatives apply specifically to operations involving lubricants and biofuel production using of vegetable oils, animal fats, where water pollution risks are carefully managed.

At the production and storage stages, a sealed system of safety basins and an oil separation unit prevent the discharge of substances into the sewage network. This infrastructure ensures that any potential leaks or spills are contained and do not pose a threat to surrounding water bodies. The facilities which operate within an industrial zone equipped with a sewage network, are connecting to a municipal waste treatment unit. Compliance with wastewater discharge standards is monitored according to environmental permit conditions, ensuring that water pollution risks are mitigated.

### RES activities

The Group's commitment to water pollution prevention in RES aligns with its overall environmental strategy, ensuring that all energy projects, including both conventional and renewable sources, adhere to high sustainability standards.



<!-- Report Page 190 -->
### 4.1.2 Targets related to Pollution [E2-3]

Regarding air and water emissions, the MOTOR OIL Group has not established measurable or resultsoriented targets. The relevant business operations in these areas are subject to regulations that define the permissible limits and specify the technologies to be employed. These regulatory requirements set specific environmental performance obligations that the Group must adhere to, ensuring alignment with legislative standards for pollution control and mitigation.

MOTOR OIL Group remains committed to sustainable industrial practices, regulatory compliance and proactive stakeholder collaboration. By prioritizing water resource protection in all its operations, the Group sets a high standard for environmental responsibility in the energy and refining industry.

#### Methodological approach

The refinery's approach to environmental monitoring has evolved over time, driven by technological advancements, operational improvements and environmental measures. Continuous enhancements in monitoring systems ensure compliance with regulatory requirements while minimizing the environmental footprint of operations. By integrating advanced technologies and refining internal procedures, the refinery maintains high standards of environmental responsibility.

Effluent monitoring involves multiple layers of analysis to safeguard water resources. According to environmental term's frequency, sampling is conducted in accordance with EN standards as defined in Decision 2014/738/EU, to evaluate water quality and detect any deviations from regulatory thresholds. The analysis is performed by MOTOR OIL's accredited laboratory or equivalent third party.

To maintain regulatory compliance and uphold environmental performance, the refinery follows a systematic approach to data management. This process involves the collection, verification and processing of environmental data to ensure accuracy and reliability. The integration of automated monitoring technologies further strengthens the ability to track key environmental parameters in real time.

The final step in the refinery's environmental monitoring process is reporting. These reports serve as a vital tool for regulatory authorities, stakeholders and internal management, demonstrating the refinery's commitment to environmental sustainability and continuous improvement. The emissions to air and water reported are based on continuous measurements where available and to calculations derived from international bibliography.

The KPI's presented are the pollutant emissions to air and water from refinery and subsidiaries (LPC, CORAL GAS, VERD) subject to European Pollutant Release and Transfer Register Regulation 166/2006/EU that exceed the legislative threshold (Annex I of Regulation). They are based on continuous measurements and calculations. Emission factors used for the calculations of air pollutants, derive from Concawe's report no. 4.19 "Air pollutant emission estimation methods for E-PRTR reporting by refineries". Calibration of automated systems for air pollutants is conducted as stated in Environmental Terms. Water pollutants' calculation was based on average/year concentration and effluents discharge flow measured. Metrics are externally validated by third party on annual basis under Eco Management and Audit Scheme (EMAS). Air emissions of CH, and N2O (eligible under Regulation 166/2006/EU) are disclosed in ESRS El metrics.

# 5 WATER AND MARINE RESOURCES [E3]

## 5.1 Impact, risk and opportunity management

### 5.1.1 Policies and actions related to water and marine resources [E3-1] [E3-2]

MOTOR OIL Group has implemented a comprehensive set of environmental policies and operational protocols that govern the sustainable use, management and protection of water and marine resources across its value chain. While the Group has not yet adopted a standalone water and marine resources policy, these topics are fully embedded within the Group's ISO 14001-certified Environmental Management Systems (EMS) and aligned with applicable EU and national environmental legislation.

The Group's policies are shaped by compliance with the EU Industrial Emissions Directive (2010/75/EU), the Water Framework Directive (2000/60/EC) and relevant permitting requirements across jurisdictions. They apply to all water-intensive operations - including refining, energy generation, waste management and environmental services - and are designed to ensure responsible sourcing, treatment, reuse and discharge of water, while protecting surrounding ecosystems, particularly marine and coastal environments near port operations.

The Group's strategic objective is to ensure the sustainable management and protection of water resources, in line with relevant EU directives (2000/60/EU), national legislation and voluntary disclosure frameworks such as GRI 303 and CDP Water. This includes improving water efficiency, minimizing pollution risks, reducing dependence on freshwater sources and enhancing resilience to climate-related water risks such as flooding, drought and sea level rise.

Recognizing evolving expectations under the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD) and the European Sustainability Reporting Standards (ESRS), MOTOR OIL Group is committed to:

· Developing a formal water and marine transition plan, consolidating current actions and establishing quantifiable objectives and timelines.

· Aligning with the Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosures (TNFD) and future science-based target frameworks for freshwater.. Exploring collaborative water governance models, especially in water-stressed areas or where shared resource use with communities and ecosystems is material.

### Refinery, Supply and Trading activities

MOTOR OIL Group's environmental policies governing water and marine resources are most advanced and deeply embedded within its refining operations, particularly at the Corinth Refinery, the Group's largest industrial site. These policies are grounded in compliance with EU and national environmental regulations, including the Industrial Emissions Directive (2010/75/EU) and the Water Framework Directive (2000/60/EC - national law 3199/2003) and are implemented through a robust ISO 14001-certified Environmental Management System (EMS).

Recognizing the refinery's substantial water needs, the Group has taken decisive steps to enhance water efficiency and reduce environmental pressure. A central pillar of this effort is the facility's dedicated desalination unit, which now supplies 100% of the refinery's freshwater needs, completely eliminating dependence on freshwater resources. This shift not only safeguards local water reserves but also underscores the Group's commitment to long-term resource conservation and operational resilience.

The Group's water management actions at the refinery extend well beyond sourcing. It includes advanced wastewater treatment systems that ensure all industrial and sanitary effluents are processed to meet or exceed strict environmental standards before being discharged.

The closed drainage network channels all effluents to the Industrial Wastewater Treatment Plant (IWWTP) and to sanitary plant, ensuring that discharges into the marine environment are controlled, treated and compliant.

The refinery also conducts periodical coastal water monitoring to assess potential impacts on marine biodiversity. This goes beyond regulatory compliance and reflects a commitment to preserving local ecosystems through scientific monitoring, adaptive management and continuous improvement.

Key refinery's water management related actions include:. Reuse of acidic process water in the crude oil desalination phase, reducing both consumption and waste.. Closed-loop cooling systems in new processing units minimize water usage while maintaining operational efficiency.. Periodical monitoring of marine water quality, recording changes in physical parameters - an approach aligned with the EU Water Framework Directive.. Integration ofindustrial and sanitary wastewater treatment, ensuring safe discharge and environmental protection.

Please note that no significant financial resources were allocated to these actions during reporting period. In addition, the Group actively participates in international marine protection efforts, including collaboration with oil spill response organization. Measures such as vessel deballasting protocols, enclosed wastewater systems and separating oily and non-oily waste are embedded into operational practices to mitigate pollution risks.

### Retail activities

Within the Retail and Fuels Marketing segment, MOTOR OIL Group applies a risk-based, proportionate approach to water and marine resource management, recognizing that water consumption in this business line is relatively low. Although no material water-related risks have been identified in the Group's retail operations - given their focus on fuel distribution, storage and consumer services - sitelevel procedures ensure that wastewater handling, drainage and spill prevention are well managed and aligned with regulatory expectations.

Key priorities include:. Compliance with wastewater discharge regulations at service stations, terminals and depots, particularly where activities such as vehicle washing or fuel handling may pose local water pollution risks.. Oil-water separation systems, drainage network controls and containment bunds at fuel terminals and storage facilities.. Integration of marine and water sustainability measures into operational frameworks such as Shell's HSSE MS (Health, Safety, Security and Environment Management System), which provides structured environmental controls for Shell-branded activities under the Group.

Specific improvements demonstrate the Group's commitment to continuous improvement in this area:. At CORAL, targeted infrastructure upgrades are underway, including the redesign of the drainage system at the Ag. Theodoroi oil terminal and the installation of new lining systems at the Alexandroupolis terminal's bunded tank farm area. These enhancements aim to reduce contamination risks and ensure safe waste handling in high-traffic, high-risk fuel storage zones.. CORAL GAS supports these efforts with systematic water consumption monitoring, helping to optimize water use even in low-intensity operations.. Across all retail sites, staff are trained in spill prevention, emergency response protocols and wastewater management, ensuring that local-level environmental protection measures are consistently applied.

<!-- Report Page 196 -->
### Power & Gas activities

In the Power and Gas segment, MOTOR OIL Group applies a precautionary and forward-looking approach to water management, recognizing the role water can play in cooling, auxiliary processes and environmental compliance - particularly in thermal power generation.

A prime example of the Group's evolving water management in this area is the THERMOILEKTRIKI KOMOTINIS power plant, currently in its commissioning phase. As part of its early operational readiness strategy, THERMOILEKTRIKI is conducting systematic recording of water inflows and outflows to establish a robust baseline for water use efficiency and discharge management once the plant becomes fully operational. This data will inform future targets, and infrastructure needs while supporting compliance with environmental permitting obligations.

### Circular Economy & Alternative Fuel Activities

While water usage in these activities is generally lower compared to refining or energy production, the Group remains committed to optimizing water use, minimizing wastewater generation and integrating sustainable practices across operations. These principles are reflected in the environmental management frameworks and day-to-day processes of several key subsidiaries, including LPC, PRASINO LADI, VERD and THALIS Environmental Services.

At LPC, the company addresses water consumption challenges through targeted monitoring and efficiency planning. A specific key performance indicator (KPI) - water consumption per metric ton of raw material processed - is used to evaluate effectiveness and guide decision-making. In addition to tracking usage, LPC also recycles a portion of its wastewater, reducing net consumption and supporting circular resource use within its production cycle.

PRASINO LADI, emphasizes continuous evaluation of water use as part of its operational routine. This monitoring allows for ongoing improvements in both efficiency and conservation, helping to reduce environmental impact while maintaining production quality. The company's commitment to responsible water use is embedded in its broader goal of advancing sustainable fuel alternatives.

At VERD, water stewardship is tightly integrated into the company's production processes. A dedicated water recovery unit, which includes distillation for volatile organic compound (VOC) removal, enables the reuse of processed water within the facility. This system not only reduces water withdrawals but also minimizes wastewater discharge volumes - contributing meaningfully to the Group's overarching commitment to circularity and environmental performance.

THALIS Environmental Services, while not yet operating under a formal, standalone water management policy, adheres to an ISO 14001-certified Environmental Management System. Through this system, THALIS systematically records water consumption across both office and operational sites. This structured monitoring ensures that even lower-intensity water users contribute to the Group's broader sustainability goals by applying efficient and responsible resource use practices.

### RES activities

MORE's core focus lies in the development and operation of solar, wind and other renewable energy assets, which typically involve low-intensity water use.

Water and marine resource considerations are incorporated into site selection, permitting and operational planning. This includes ensuring soil and land stability to reduce the risk of runoff and erosion, especially in sloped or sensitive terrains. For large-scale solar installations, attention is given to stormwater management infrastructure to prevent local flooding and protect adjacent land and water systems.

In wind and solar project development, the Group takes deliberate measures to avoid sensitive aquatic ecosystems, such as wetlands, riparian zones and protected habitats, ensuring that projects are

designed and implemented in a manner that aligns with both regulatory expectations and biodiversity conservation principles.

### 5.1.2 Targets related to water and marine resources [E3-3]

While quantified, time-bound water and marine resource targets are not yet in place, the Group is actively advancing toward their definition by building the necessary data infrastructure, operational benchmarks and governance processes. Next year, MOTOR OIL Group will enter into a target-setting phase, during which site-specific and activity-based water targets will be formally developed. These targets will address key performance areas such as water use efficiency, recycling and reuse and discharge quality, tailored to the operational context of each business segment. In parallel, the Group will begin to assess the applicability of science-based target methodologies for freshwater, incorporating them where relevant and as global standards continue to mature.

The Group continues to track the effectiveness of its water related actions and intends to further strengthen the process.

Looking ahead, the Group has identified a number of forward-looking priorities to support a structured water transition pathway. These include:

· Upgrading monitoring infrastructure and data reliability across all sites by 2025.. Standardizing water accounting and integrating recently acquired waste management businesses (e.g., THALIS).. Expanding internal verification and exploring third-party assurance of water data.

· Developing performance indicators such as water intensity metrics and recycling rates, with the aim of evaluating formal target-setting by 2026.. Reduction in freshwater dependency from the operation of the desalination unit by continuing enhancement of circular water use.

# 6 BIODIVERSITY AND ECOSYSTEMS [E4]

## 6.1 Strategy

### 6.1.1 Transition Plan and Consideration of Biodiversity and Ecosystems in Strategy and Business Model [E4-1]

MOTOR OIL Group's transformation strategy into a diversified multi-energy and circular economy leader involves a wide range of activities that interact with nature and biodiversity.

MOTOR OIL Group's transition plan includes an ongoing evaluation of biodiversity-related risks and dependencies across Group's value chain. The Group is systematically reviewing its policies to incorporate measurable biodiversity objectives and integrating international best practices into operational planning and execution.

Informed by the Intergovernmental Science-Policy Platform on Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services (IPBES), MOTOR OIL Group's biodiversity strategy addresses both direct and indirect drivers of biodiversity loss, including land-use change, climate change, invasive species, pollution and economic incentives. MOTOR OIL Group's approach is built on continuous monitoring, adaptive management and investment in nature-based solutions - such as using native plant species for reforestation, promoting agroforestry and advancing regenerative agriculture practices.

The biodiversity plan covers activities across renewable energy development, biofuels and e-fuels production, carbon capture and low-carbon hydrogen, circular economy and waste management, land stewardship, infrastructure expansion.

The Group is also embedding biodiversity dependencies and risk assessments into its climate resilience planning, ensuring that nature conservation efforts are fully integrated into the broader climate strategy. Group's methodology draws on global frameworks and science-based approaches, including those of the IPCC (Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change) and the IEA (International Energy Agency), to align biodiversity action with climate mitigation and adaptation goals.

Another central component of the strategy is the Group's Forest Program, which supports biodiversity and climate goals through the following key initiatives:

1\. Collection, processing and storage of forest seeds through its ginning plant to support reforestation and ecological restoration.

2\. Forestry nurseries for the propagation of native species to be used in reforestation and rehabilitation projects.

3\. Afforestation and reforestation initiatives aimed restoring degraded ecosystems in Groups business activities.

4\. Forest protection, preservation and conservation aiming at preventing both planned and unplanned deforestation and degradation.

5\. Revegetation and soil restoration to support ecosystem recovery and improve biodiversity in impacted landscapes. (Evaluated)

6\. Implementation of regenerative agriculture and sustainable land management to enhance biodiversity, soil quality and landscape resilience. (Evaluated)

A further cornerstone of MOTOR OIL Group's transition plan is active participation in EU and national co-funded programs that align with its biodiversity and sustainability objectives. These include projects focused on biodiversity restoration, renewable energy expansion and reforestation, all aimed at improving ecosystem resilience and natural capital.

#### 6.1.2 Material Impacts, Risks and Opportunities and their interaction with Strategy and Business Model [SBM-3]

The establishment of refineries, storage facilities and transport infrastructure such as pipelines may lead to, altering critical wildlife habitats and reducing biodiversity by converting natural areas into industrial zones. This underscores the importance of a structured environmental management approach (Ecological Assessment through Environmental Impact Assessment) that integrates biodiversity risk assessments into project planning and operations. Given the potential severity of pollution incidents, comprehensive spill prevention and response planning are critical in mitigating ecological disruptions.

Managing RES infrastructures and other assets located in protected or high biodiversity areas poses a notable risk to biodiversity. During both the setup and operational phases of renewable energy activities, there is a risk of negative impacts on particular species and the area's overall biodiversity richness and/or abundance. Recognizing this, MORE ensures that all renewable energy projects comply with Greek legislation and undergo environmental assessments before construction and operation. While renewable energy developments aim to produce green energy, their placement in protected areas requires careful planning to prevent adverse effects.

Any potential oil spills and other incidents with significant impacts, including damage to marine and terrestrial ecosystems, are generally recognized as risks for the Oil & Gas Industry.

No parts of the refinery operate in natural habitats or protected areas that might be affected by Group's activities. MOTOR OIL Group's operations are conducted within designated industrial zones, with strict environmental controls in place to prevent biodiversity impacts. Nonetheless, given the industry's reliance on marine and terrestrial ecosystems, the Group actively engages in biodiversity protection measures. MOTOR OIL Group's commitment extends beyond refinery operations to renewable energy projects, retail fuel networks and marine transport activities. In this way, the Group integrates biodiversity considerations across its entire operations, ensuring that both mitigation and restoration efforts contribute to long-term environmental sustainability. The Group's subsidiaries operating in fuels retail, including CORAL and AVIN, follow similar environmental management principles. This cross-sectoral approach ensures that MOTOR OIL Group's sustainability initiatives remain consistent, whether in mitigating risks from oil spills or enhancing ecosystem resilience through reforestation efforts.

Forest programs that include nurseries and reforestation efforts, along with ginning plants, positively impact ecosystems by restoring habitats and biodiversity and enhancing carbon sequestration. These initiatives align with Group's broader commitment to biodiversity protection, reinforcing its structured approach to environmental risk management.

## Risks

Land degradation caused by industrial infrastructure development presents material financial risks, including costly environmental restoration, regulatory penalties and reputational damage among environmentally conscious stakeholders. The conversion of natural landscapes into industrial zones disrupts ecosystems, leading to biodiversity loss and long-term ecological damage. Companies operating in such environments must implement strict environmental controls and sustainable land-use planning to mitigate these risks.

The potential severity of pollution incidents, particularly in marine and terrestrial ecosystems, necessitates comprehensive spill prevention and response planning. Industrial operations, including refineries and fuel storage facilities, pose inherent risks to biodiversity due to potential spills, emissions and habitat deterioration. To mitigate these risks, the Group has developed structured environmental risk management strategies that include continuous monitoring, emergency response protocols and engagement with specialized spill response organizations.

These measures not only help prevent environmental harm but also reduce financial exposure by ensuring regulatory compliance and limiting the costs associated with pollution-related liabilities.

MORE's Renewable energy projects, while critical to producing green energy, also present biodiversity risks, particularly when developed in protected or ecologically sensitive areas. Wind parks, for example, can disrupt local wildlife habitats, leading to conflicts with conservation efforts. The financial implications of such risks include potential regulatory fines, costly mitigation efforts and reputational challenges among stakeholders who expect renewable energy to align with broader environmental goals.

<!-- Report Page 202 -->
## Biodiversity sites

MOTOR OIL Group's operations interact with biodiversity-sensitive areas primarily through its renewable energy subsidiary, MORE, which operates wind farms within protected areas. While Group's refinery, fuels marketing and infrastructure projects do not directly impact protected ecosystems, its renewable energy assets require structured biodiversity risk management which is integrated in the Group's ERM system and conservation measures to ensure compliance with environmental regulations.

Regarding MORE's operation sites, habitat protection and biodiversity conservation are key considerations. These locations have been developed in compliance with Greek environmental legislation and all projects undergo strict environmental and ecological impact assessments (EIA) before approval. As part of these requirements, biodiversity management plans ensure that any temporary environmental disturbances during the construction and operational phases are properly addressed. During the reporting period, wind production farm of "Paliovoros - Sanida - Listi" of 22.50 MW was under construction.

The following wind farms are located within or near protected sites:

· Tsitomi Wind Farm (Delphi, 23.00 MW) - Located 1.6 km inside the boundaries of a Natura 2000 area (GR2450009). The affected forest areas during construction have been restored and native plant species were replanted under the guidance of forestry authorities.

· Kato Lakomata Wind Farm (Dorida, 19.20 MW) -Located 1.2km inside a Natura 2000 area (GR2450004). Forested areas impacted during construction were restored following an environmental restoration study.

· Psaromyta Wind Farm (Dorida, 9.60 MW) - Located 3.5 km inside a Natura 2000 area (GR2450004). Reforestation efforts ensured the recovery of affected vegetation.. Toumba - Anthovouni Wind Farm (Florina, 28.90 MW) - In close proximity to Natura 2000 sites GR1340003 and GR1340009. Habitat restoration efforts were conducted for impacted areas.. Kalogerovouni Wind Farm (Lakonia, 17.1 MW) - Located within Natura 2000 sites GR2540001 and GR2540007 SPA, with restored forested areas.. Terpandros Wind Farm (Lesvos Island, 4.80 MW) - Situated within Natura 2000 site GR4110100 SPA, with compliance to Environmental Terms Approval (ETA).

· Antissa Wind Farm (Lesvos Island, 4.20 MW) - Located within Natura 2000 area GR4110100 SPA, where impacted forest land was restored under strict conservation measures.

· Lyrkeio Wind Farm (Arkadia-Argolida, 39.60 MW) - Located inside a Natura 2000 area (GR2510004), where post-construction restoration was completed following national forestry guidelines.

· Agia Dynati & Extension Wind Farm (Kefalonia, 34.50 MW) - Within Natura 2000 site GR2220006 SPA, with reforestation conducted according to a specialized restoration study.. Magoula Kazakou & Extension Wind Farm (Evros, 39.1 MW) - Located near the boundaries of Natura 2000 sites GR111003 SPA and GR1110100 SPA.

## 6.2 Impact, risk and opportunity management

### 6.2.1 Policies and Actions related to Biodiversity and Ecosystems [E4-2] [E4-3]

Biodiversity conservation and responsible environmental management are fundamental aspects of MOTOR OIL Group's operations. To strengthen commitment to sustainability, MOTOR OIL Group has established a Biodiversity Policy, approved by the Board of Directors, which applies to all subsidiaries. This policy is approved by the sustainability committee and aligns with the regulatory framework and best international practices. It defines Group's approach to minimizing negative impacts on biodiversity, integrating sustainability principles into Group's business model and contributing to the preservation and restoration of natural ecosystems. Through Group's stakeholder engagement plan, the affected communities and environmental authorities are engaged in the planning process, in order to identify potential social consequences that can be derived from the project development such as minimizing land disturbance and habitat fragmentation.

The Biodiversity Policy provides a framework to integrate biodiversity considerations into strategic and operational decision-making. It defines MOTOR OIL Group's commitment to avoiding and minimizing biodiversity loss through risk management and impact mitigation strategies, incorporating biodiversity management into the Group's Environmental Management System (EMS) to assess and monitor risks effectively, applying the mitigation hierarchy (avoid, minimize, restore, compensate) across all new and existing infrastructure projects, aiming for sustainable land use by avoiding new developments in protected biodiversity-sensitive areas where possible, promoting research, awareness and stakeholder engagement to enhance biodiversity protection and aligning with EU biodiversity standards, including the EU Taxonomy Regulation's "Do No Significant Harm" principles.

The policy applies to all subsidiaries and operational areas of the Group. It governs biodiversity considerations across all projects, stating that actions for sustainable agriculture, oceans, or forestry comply with the requirements set in national legislation. The Sustainability Committee is responsible for overseeing the implementation of the policy, while the Sustainability Division ensures its communication across all business functions. The policy commits to Greek and European biodiversity protection laws, including Law 3937/2011 on the Protection of Nature and Biodiversity, Presidential Decree 67/1981 on Wild Bird Conservation and Environmental Licensing Law 4014/2011, which includes Ministerial Decisions for Biodiversity Impact Assessments.

The Group integrates stakeholder engagement into biodiversity planning, collaborating with government agencies, NGOs, local communities and scientific institutions to ensure inclusive and science-based biodiversity management. For example, this includes partnerships with environmental authorities for permitting and biodiversity impact assessments, joint initiatives on habitat restoration and species protection, as well as engagement with local communities through stakeholder representative discussions to promote transparency, incorporate local knowledge and ensure social acceptance of projects.

Awareness and training programs are designed to address employees and subcontractors to ensure policy adherence. Any stakeholder will be able to access it once will be publicly available and internal dissemination will be conducted through employee training, newsletters and official documentation channels.

By adopting biodiversity offsets, habitat restoration efforts and nature-based solutions, MOTOR OIL Group aims to reduce environmental impacts while promoting sustainable development. Specifically, until know the Group through the operation of the forestry nurseries has managed to develop a capacity to produce 3.5 million plants. Through reforestation activities has already planted trees across 52 hectares of fire affected areas to support habitat regeneration.

Across all operational pillars, MOTOR OIL Group has implemented environmental impact mitigation plans aligned with regulatory requirements. Specific initiatives have been designed based on the nature of each business activity, ensuring that biodiversity considerations are addressed effectively.

<!-- Report Page 204 -->
### Supply, Refining & Trading activities

Although the refinery itself is located within an industrial zone and is not classified as a biodiversitysensitive area, the associated port facilities present potential biodiversity risks, particularly in relation to coastal and marine ecosystems. These ecosystems are vulnerable to impacts such as oil spills, waste discharge and habitat disturbance. As such, the Group has integrated biodiversity protection measures into both its refining operations and port-related activities.

MOTOR OIL Group refining operations are governed by a certified ISO 14001 Environmental Management System, which ensures best environmental practices are followed. MOTOR OIL Group has developed a comprehensive Facility Contingency Plan that complies with national legislation and international guidelines, focused on spill prevention, emergency preparedness and environmental protection. This includes collaboration with global response partners, regular emergency drills and strict anticontamination protocols aimed at preventing damage to marine biodiversity.

The port facilities, due to their proximity to sensitive marine habitats, are subject to enhanced environmental risk management. The Group has identified and classified environmental risks at key port sites and implemented site-specific Operations Manuals, along with targeted plans for risk prevention, mitigation and emergency response. This includes a structured waste reception and handling plan for ship-generated waste and cargo residues, ensuring compliance with international marine pollution regulations such as MARPOL.

In addition, the Group integrates Best Available Techniques (BAT) to minimize land degradation and reduce unintended biodiversity impacts in both terrestrial and marine zones.

### Fuel Retail activities

In the Group's retail operations, potential biodiversity risks stem primarily from fuel storage, transportation and distribution activities. To address these risks, the Group has implemented preventive environmental measures, including a structured spill response strategy at fuel depots. CORAL and AVIN have established an annual drill program, simulating spill containment and response scenarios, often in collaboration with local authorities. Additionally, an emergency response vehicle is deployed for rapid intervention in case of spills to prevent potential contamination of soil and nearby water bodies.

Subsidiaries involved in fuel distribution and alternative energy production have adopted waste minimization strategies to reduce hazardous material use in service stations and office operations. CORAL GAS has implemented LPG recovery initiatives, ensuring that non-compliant cartridges and cylinders are processed efficiently to prevent environmental contamination.

### RES activities

Recognizing biodiversity related challenges, MORE ensures that all renewable energy projects undergo rigorous environmental assessments before construction and operation, balancing clean energy expansion with ecosystem preservation. Through environmental assessments' output, the Group aims to align its renewable energy initiatives with national biodiversity conservation goals while maintaining regulatory compliance and stakeholder confidence. MORE's operations within or nearby protected areas, require strict biodiversity impact assessments and mitigation strategies. To ensure sustainable operation within these sensitive ecosystems, the Group has developed a biodiversity monitoring and habitat restoration program that aligns with Environmental Terms Approval (ETA) requirements.

Monitoring and restoration programs are conducted to track wildlife interactions with wind farm operations, with special attention to bird mortality rates due to turbine activity. For wind energy projects located near sensitive habitats, biodiversity measures considered such as restoring native vegetation around turbine bases and deploying bird detection radar systems to protect endangered species. Where necessary, it has been installed monitoring systems (DtBird) which detect birds and can optionally take two independent actions to mitigate bird collision risk: the activation of warning sounds and/or the stoppage of the WTG.

Additionally, impacted forest areas from wind farm construction undergo reforestation and restoration efforts, where native flora species are replanted following specialized ecological assessments.

As part of the Forest program which support biodiversity, in 2024 the following initiatives took place for Renewable Energy projects:. Reforestation activities at Kellas wind farm.. Ongoing maintenance of restored forest areas in Tsamadorachi, Pournari, Korobili and Kassidiaris I & II.. Compliance with reforestation fee obligations and phytotechnical restoration requirements.. Nature-based solutions, such as utilizing native plant species for reforestation and promoting agroforestry practices are integrated into reforestation efforts, ensuring long-term ecosystem resilience.

<!-- Report Page 206 -->
### Circular Economy & Alternative Fuels activities

Within MOTOR OIL Group's circular economy and alternative fuels division, including LPC and VERD waste management and alternative fuel production is integrated. It has to be noted that all installations operate in industrial areas and do not directly affect biodiversity sensitive areas.

THALIS Environmental Services ensures compliance with environmental regulations, particularly in infrastructure projects that may affect local ecosystems. THALIS environmental management framework incorporates best practices to minimize ecological disturbance during project execution.

Akeyinitiative in the Group's circular economy strategy is the Forests Program, whichsupportsreforestation, ecosystem restoration and carbon sequestration. This initiative aligns with the EU Biodiversity Strategy for 2030, promoting sustainable forest management and environmental research. The Amygdaleza ginning plant plays a central role in seed conservation, large-scale reforestation and biodiversity enhancement projects.

As the Group advances its biodiversity strategy, it remains committed to enhancing its monitoring frameworks, stakeholder collaborations and impact mitigation measures. The Biodiversity Policy serves as a guiding framework to integrate biodiversity considerations into business operations, ensuring that sustainability remains a core component of decision-making. Through continuous assessment, stakeholder engagement and adherence to international best practices, MOTOR OIL Group is dedicated to maintaining a positive environmental footprint and strengthening ecosystem resilience across its operations.

### 6.2.2 Targets related to Biodiversity and Ecosystems [E4-4]

At present, MOTOR OIL Group has not adopted measurable or results-oriented targets specifically related to biodiversity and ecosystems. This is primarily due to the complexity associated with biodiversity metrics, the challenge of establishing uniform performance indicators across a broad range of activities and limitations in current international standards and methodologies for biodiversity measurement and reporting.

Nonetheless, at MOTOR OIL Group, it integrates biodiversity protection and promotion into Group-level strategy. The Group continuously tracks and monitors the effectiveness of its actions, initiatives and policies in relation to biodiversity loss and impacts on species. MOTOR OIL Group's overarching aim is to contribute to a nature-positive society, ensuring that the operations of all Group companies support the preservation, development and regeneration of natural heritage and ecosystems.

While formal, measurable targets are not yet in place, the Group remains committed to enhancing its biodiversity strategy and aligning with emerging global frameworks, such as the Taskforce on Naturerelated Financial Disclosures (TNFD). As these frameworks mature and provide more robust guidance, the Group will evaluate the feasibility of adopting science-based and results-driven biodiversity targets in the future.
